3. declare the target object or bounded target function;
4. identify load-bearing, modulating, compressed, excluded, and uncertain source features;
5. map those features and relations to target-claim elements;
6. preserve Reference, Type, Functional, and Temporal Continuity as applicable;
7. declare five-part Loss, alternatives, Source Ceiling, Claim Ceiling, and uncertainty;
8. record local upper-bound findings;
9. route Stop, Claim Reduction, Non-Capture, or complete audit as warranted;
10. select one canonical Output Class only after all required gates are complete.

A conceptual view may compare several records, but comparison does not merge them. Each transformation occurrence retains its own source declaration, target declaration, Loss, local findings, and disposition. A chain-level result requires explicit handoff relations and later integrated testing.

```text
Traceability-Ceiling view
≠ new transformation occurrence
≠ automatic chain composite
≠ inherited result
```

The Formal Model may validate that required fields, references, enumerations, and invariants are present. It may not decide whether a source feature is genuinely load-bearing, whether a target is substantively elastic, whether compression is acceptable, or whether a dependency claim is empirically true.

```text
schema-valid trace view
≠ substantive TraceableLoad proof
```

<a id="45-10-mandatory-upper-stop-and-claim-reduction"></a>

### 45.10 Mandatory Upper Stop and Claim Reduction

The Traceability Ceiling requires two distinct responses when a target claim outruns its source trace. One response stops the current operation or route. The other narrows the claim to a residual formulation that the source can still carry. These responses are not interchangeable.

#### C45-UPPERSTOP-01 — Mandatory Upper Stop applies where source structures, relations, temporality, reference return, or functional dependence are no longer reconstructible for the current route

Mandatory Upper Stop is required when continued transformation would conceal rather than repair the upper-bound failure. Typical conditions include:

- the declared source object can no longer be identified in the target;
- load-bearing relations have disappeared and cannot be reconstructed;
- temporal formation has been flattened where order, duration, recurrence, or irreversibility is constitutive;
- a finer reconstruction cannot return to the same reference object or coarser function;
- a projected function remains unchanged under materially relevant source change without a warranted invariant;
- only metaphorical, rhetorical, or nominal continuity remains;
- current sources cannot support the stronger route and further abstraction would exceed the Source or Claim Ceiling;
- irrecoverable Loss has removed the evidence needed to test the target.

The stop is route- and claim-specific:

```text
Mandatory Upper Stop for T under C and Q
≠ permanent closure of source object S
≠ universal rejection of target vocabulary
```

A later operation may be attempted only as a new testable claim with a new record or explicit lineage, changed source basis or scope, and preserved prior disposition. A new label, additional citation, or more elaborate formalization is not re-entry by itself.

Mandatory Upper Stop is a positive result because it prevents an unsupported abstraction from acquiring apparent continuity or authority through repetition. It does not imply that the source is unreal, that no bounded claim is possible, or that every alternative route must stop.

#### C45-CLAIMREDUCTION-01 — Claim Reduction preserves a supported residual claim and remains distinct from Mandatory Upper Stop

Claim Reduction is appropriate when part of the target claim retains TraceableLoad but the stronger formulation exceeds the ceiling. It requires three explicit objects:

```text
original claim Q_strong
+ identified unsupported or untraceable excess E
+ residual claim Q_bounded
→ retest Q_bounded as a new delimited claim
```

A valid reduction must state:

- which source trace still supports the residual claim;
- which relation, range, temporal scope, target function, or generality is removed;
- which Loss and uncertainty remain;
- why the narrower claim is not merely editorially shorter;
- which gates must be rerun before a positive Output Class is possible.

Examples of bounded reduction include:

- replacing a universal macroclaim with a claim restricted to one documented temporal interval;
- narrowing a projected function to a specified context;
- separating one elastic label into two source-dependent claims;
- retaining a descriptive association while withdrawing a structural dependency claim;
- preserving a local component relation while stopping the unsupported integrated composite;
- retaining an analogy while withdrawing identity or functional projection.

```text
Claim Reduction
≠ automatic admissibility
≠ support downgrade only
≠ cosmetic qualification
```

The reduced claim must be retested. If it passes all applicable gates, the eventual mapping may be `admissible_with_bounded_claim` or another warranted canonical class. The unrevised stronger claim retains its prior upper-bound failure, Stop, or other disposition. Success of the residual claim does not erase that history.

Mandatory Upper Stop and Claim Reduction may occur together at different claim levels. The strong route may stop while a narrower residual claim is registered for retest. They remain separate records or clearly separated claim dispositions.

```text
stop Q_strong
+ register Q_bounded for retest
≠ Q_strong repaired
```

Chapter 51 owns the integrated Stop, Claim Reduction, and re-entry procedure across operations and chains. Chapter 45 establishes only the upper-bound trigger and the minimum distinction.

<a id="45-11-output-mapping-and-handoff"></a>

### 45.11 Output Mapping and Handoff

Local Traceability-Ceiling findings do not automatically determine a final result. `load-bearing trace present`, `elasticity pressure`, `source limited`, `upper-bound failure`, `Mandatory Upper Stop`, and `Claim Reduction required` are local findings or routing burdens. They must be integrated with the remaining admissibility conditions before one canonical Output Class is selected.

#### C45-OUTPUTMAPPING-01 — local upper-bound findings map only to the ten canonical Output Classes and only after all applicable checks

The closed inventory remains:

```text
admissible
admissible_with_bounded_claim
admissible_but_provisional
resolution_neutral
analogy_only
partially_admissible
claim_reduction_required
mandatory_stop
failed_transformation
non_capture
```

Possible mappings remain conditional:

- a fully traceable target that passes all other gates may be `admissible`;
- a retested narrower claim may be `admissible_with_bounded_claim`;
- a material source limitation with bounded support may contribute to `admissible_but_provisional`;
- a useful resemblance without constitutive dependency may be `analogy_only`;
- a mixed object with separately retained traceable and untraceable components may require `partially_admissible`;
- an unrevised overclaim with a supported residual formulation may require `claim_reduction_required`;
- a route whose source load cannot be reconstructed and cannot be repaired within scope may require `mandatory_stop` or `failed_transformation` depending on the complete audit;
- an inadequacy of the current grammar or target function may require `non_capture`.

No local phrase becomes a new class:

```text
macro-label elasticity
source limitation
upper-bound failure
trace retained
Claim Reduction route
Upper Stop trigger
≠ new Output Classes
```

The pressure packet remains protected:

```yaml
U0:
  status: citation_sufficiency_statement_not_accepted

T:
  result: unadjudicated

TL:
  result: unadjudicated

TE:
  result: unadjudicated

TS:
  result: unadjudicated

upper_bound:
  result_selected: false
  route_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
  mandatory_upper_stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
```

WP3 does not infer a result from the synthetic packet. It completes the method by which a future instantiated record could be tested.

#### C45-HANDOFF46-53-01 — Chapter 45 hands remaining burdens to Chapters 46–53 without pre-empting their integrated procedures

The ownership sequence remains:

```text
Chapter 46
→ full Counterfactual Sensitivity procedure and six values

Chapter 47
→ integrated Reference, Type, Functional, and Temporal Continuity

Chapter 48
→ Selection, Compression, Exclusion, Uncertainty, and Loss

Chapter 49
→ Source Ceiling and Calibration Boundary

Chapter 50
→ Anti-Immunization

Chapter 51
→ integrated Stop, Claim Reduction, and re-entry

Chapter 52
→ Non-Capture

Chapter 53
→ integrated twelve-stage audit
```

Chapter 45 supplies source-trace, dependency, elasticity, non-exhaustiveness, and upper-bound routing burdens to these chapters. It does not decide empirical truth, causality, semantic validity, normative legitimacy, application authority, or person-level diagnosis.

The formal boundary remains:

```text
TraceableLoad
≠ truth proof
≠ causal proof
≠ authority inheritance
```

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

#### C45-HANDOFF-01 — WP3 hands WP4 a complete upper-bound method without adjudicating the synthetic pressure packet

Chapter 45 now establishes:

- the Praxeological Traceability Ceiling as the upper relational boundary;
- minimum TraceableLoad through mapped source features, relations, temporality, costs, roles, and asymmetries;
- traceability as more than citation, documentation volume, analogy, or label fit;
- recurrent upper-bound failure forms without automatic verdicts;
- a five-position Constitutive Source Trace compatible with the canonical five-part Loss object;
- operation-specific Source-Target Dependency for `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS`;
- rival-source pressure and macro-label elasticity without pre-empting Chapter 46;
- traceability without exhaustive or lossless reproduction;
- Shared-Record mapping without a second grammar;
- Mandatory Upper Stop and Claim Reduction as distinct responses;
- conditional mapping to the ten canonical Output Classes only after complete audit;
- explicit handoff to Chapters 46–53.

The canonical formulation is:

> **Above the Praxeological Traceability Ceiling, abstraction no longer carries reconstructible praxis load. It becomes a label without traceable source structure.**

Chapter 45 remains open pending WP4. WP4 must perform the integrated Contract, redundancy, ownership, pressure-packet, inventory, model, and authority audit; verify all twenty-four duties exactly once; determine the bounded chapter-level lock; and prepare Chapter 46. It must not adjudicate `T`, `TL`, `TE`, or `TS`; select a route; assign a Counterfactual Sensitivity value; choose a pressure-object Stop or Claim Reduction; or map a pressure object to a canonical Output Class.

No Case, Test, or Smoke YAML is created. The three-operation, ten-class, sixteen-Rule, twelve-stage, six-sensitivity-value, and five-Loss-field inventories remain unchanged. `authority_inheritance` remains prohibited.

## 46. Counterfactual Sensitivity

<a id="46-1-purpose"></a>

### 46.1 Purpose

Counterfactual Sensitivity is the common STRATA load test for whether a transformation result depends on the source structures that the analysis itself declares load-bearing. It asks whether a warranted modification of one such source feature would require a corresponding change in the transformation result, the target claim, or the bounded status of that claim.

The test belongs to the Admissibility Band. It supplements the Praxeological Relevance Floor and the Praxeological Traceability Ceiling by pressuring a declared source-to-target relation rather than merely checking whether the relation has been named. Chapter 44 asks whether an added distinction changes a warranted praxis reconstruction. Chapter 45 asks whether the target still carries reconstructible source load. Chapter 46 asks whether the declared source load makes a difference under controlled variation.

```text
PraxisPurchase
+ TraceableLoad
+ declared source dependence
+ controlled variation pressure
→ possible Counterfactual Sensitivity finding
```

The sequence is non-compensatory. A strong local sensitivity finding cannot repair a missing source trace, failed TypeIntegrity, broken Reference Continuity, unsupported target function, undeclared Loss, or an exceeded Claim Ceiling. Conversely, a formally complete source trace is not sufficient where the claimed target remains indifferent to changes in the very features presented as constitutive.

```text
Counterfactual Sensitivity
≠ replacement for TraceableLoad
≠ replacement for TypeIntegrity
≠ replacement for continuity
≠ complete admissibility result
```

The test is also not a causal tribunal. It does not establish causal necessity, identify a universal law, recover an unobserved history, prove what would actually have happened, or isolate one cause from all background conditions. It tests the internal and source-bounded dependence burden of a declared STRATA claim.

```text
source-dependence load test
≠ causal proof
≠ empirical experiment
≠ historical alternate-world reconstruction
```

This distinction matters because a STRATA transformation may be structurally dependent without supporting a full causal claim. A composite may depend on the order of constitutive events without proving that this order alone caused the later outcome. A decomposition may identify a component whose removal would alter the reconstructed function without showing that the component is causally sufficient. A projection may depend on one source relation for a bounded target function without proving that this relation produces the function in every context.

Counterfactual Sensitivity therefore remains claim-relative and context-bound. Its object is not reality in general but one declared transformation occurrence, one source baseline, one relevant modification, one expected target response, and one Claim Scope.

```text
sensitivity of T under C for Q
≠ universal sensitivity of X
```

The test may produce strong, partial, weak, insensitive, underdetermined, or untestable local findings. Those six values are developed in §§46.4–46.8. They are neither ordered ranks nor canonical Output Classes. Chapter 46 WP1 establishes only the purpose, core question, admissible modification burden, baseline, expected-change requirement, and source discipline needed before any value can be selected.

#### C46-PURPOSE-01 — Counterfactual Sensitivity is a supplementary source-dependence load test

The governing methodological claim is:

> **Counterfactual Sensitivity tests whether a transformation result depends on the source features declared constitutive, strongly modulating, temporally load-bearing, or functionally necessary. It supplements but does not replace TraceableLoad, TypeIntegrity, continuity, Loss disclosure, Contextual Boundedness, or the complete admissibility audit.**

This claim preserves the ownership sequence:

```text
Chapter 45
→ declares and reconstructs source-target load

Chapter 46
→ pressures that declared dependence under controlled variation

Chapter 47 and later LIMITS chapters
→ test continuity, Loss, calibration, immunity, Stop, Non-Capture, and integrated admissibility
```

A sensitivity test cannot be used to skip the earlier declarations or the later checks. If the source feature was never identified, if the target claim is unstable, or if the reference object has shifted, the analyst does not possess a meaningful counterfactual test merely by writing an imagined variation.

The formal boundary remains:

```text
formal counterfactual field completion
≠ real sensitivity result
≠ causal truth
≠ authority inheritance
```

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

<a id="46-2-core-question"></a>

### 46.2 Core Question

The canonical question is:

> **Would a relevant change in a declared load-bearing source structure alter the transformation result?**

The question contains four separate burdens:

1. **Would** — the test concerns a bounded expected dependence, not a narrated certainty about an unrealized world.
2. **Relevant change** — the modification must pressure a source feature that the claim actually treats as constitutive or materially modulating.
3. **Declared load-bearing source structure** — the baseline feature must already be identified in the Shared Transformation Record and supported by the available source basis.
4. **Alter the transformation result** — the analyst must state what target element, function, relation, scope, confidence, or disposition is expected to change.

Each burden must remain explicit. If any one is missing, the test becomes too elastic to discriminate between dependence, coincidence, analogy, label persistence, and unsupported speculation.

```text
named variation
≠ relevant variation

relevant variation
≠ expected target response declared

expected target response declared
≠ response empirically established
```

The core question is not answered by stating that “the outcome would be different.” A defensible test must identify the particular result element and the expected claim effect. Depending on the operation and the claim, the expected response may be:

- a different composite boundary;
- a changed ordering or relation within a composite;
- loss of a claimed source function;
- weakening or removal of a projected target function;
- narrowing of Claim Scope;
- separation of one broad claim into several bounded claims;
- a shift from a substantive claim to `analogy_only`;
- Claim Reduction, Stop, or Non-Capture pressure;
- no warranted target change, which later requires interpretation rather than automatic robustness.

A meaningful counterfactual test therefore has the minimum shape:

```text
baseline source S0
+ load-bearing feature F
+ relevant modification M(F)
+ target claim Q0
+ expected target response ΔQ
→ candidate sensitivity test
```

This shape does not presume the result. It creates the burden that later sections will evaluate.

#### C46-COREQUESTION-01 — The test asks whether controlled source change requires target change

The core operational formulation is:

```text
Given transformation occurrence T,
source baseline S0,
declared feature F,
bounded modification M,
target claim Q0,
and context C:

Would M(F) require Q0 to change,
narrow, separate, weaken, fail,
or lose its claimed function?
```

The analyst must also preserve the possibility that more than one response remains plausible or that no source-bounded response can be supported. The question is therefore not a binary device that forces “changes” or “does not change.” It opens a controlled response space that later supports the six canonical sensitivity values.

The test remains local. A finding for one target claim does not automatically extend to another claim made from the same source object. The same source modification may strongly affect a projected function, weakly affect a composite description, and leave an unrelated decomposition claim untouched.

```text
same source modification
+ different target claims
→ potentially different sensitivity findings
```

Likewise, the same target label used in different Frames or contexts does not establish one common counterfactual test. Each declared function and Claim Scope must be tested against its own source relation.

#### C46-BASELINE-01 — Every test requires an explicit baseline and analytical coordinates

Before variation, the record must state at least:

```yaml
counterfactual_baseline:
  reference_object:
  transformation_occurrence:
  operation_type:
  source_baseline:
  load_bearing_feature:
  frame:
  source_granularity:
  target_granularity:
  relative_level_where_relevant:
  temporal_scope:
  transformation_context:
  target_claim:
  claim_scope:
  claim_ceiling:
  source_basis:
```

This is a conceptual view of existing Shared Transformation Record paths. It does not create a new top-level record object or schema field.

The baseline prevents four common substitutions:

```text
new reference object
≠ modified feature of same source object

new Frame
≠ counterfactual change within prior Frame

new target function
≠ altered result of prior projection

new claim
≠ revised version of prior claim automatically
```

Where the analyst intentionally changes the Frame, reference object, operation, target function, or Claim Scope, that change creates a new testable claim. It may be legitimate, but it cannot be used to claim that the original counterfactual burden has been answered.

<a id="46-3-relevant-source-modification"></a>

### 46.3 Relevant Source Modification

A Counterfactual Sensitivity test may not vary anything merely because the variation is imaginable. It must modify a feature whose relation to the target has already been declared and source-supported as one of the following:

- **constitutive** — without the feature or relation, the declared object or function would no longer be the same relevant analytical object;
- **strongly modulating** — the feature materially shapes degree, boundary, distribution, direction, or persistence of the target claim;
- **temporally load-bearing** — order, duration, repetition, delay, Non-Event, sedimentation, or irreversibility carries part of the claim;
- **functionally necessary** — the bounded target function depends on the feature under the declared context and Claim Scope.

```text
admissible modification target
→ declared source load
→ source-supported relation to Q
```

Incidental properties, arbitrary labels, convenient paraphrases, decorative classifications, and source features unrelated to the target claim do not create a valid test. Changing them may leave the target unchanged for the trivial reason that the claim never depended on them.

```text
incidental variation
+ unchanged target
≠ robustness finding
```

The modification must also remain within the source horizon. The analyst may abstract from a documented alternative, remove a supported component, alter an evidenced ordering relation, vary a bounded value within an observed or source-warranted range, or compare materially distinct source configurations where the record supports the comparison. The analyst may not invent a history, actor capacity, event, Non-Event, operator occurrence, or structural relation merely to make the target respond.

#### C46-RELEVANTSOURCEMOD-01 — Admissible modifications pressure declared source load

The relevance test is:

```text
F declared load-bearing for Q
+ M changes F or its constitutive relation
+ M remains source-bounded
→ relevant modification candidate
```

A modification fails this threshold where:

- `F` was never declared load-bearing;
- `F` is supported only as incidental background;
- the modification changes terminology but not structure;
- the variation exceeds available source knowledge;
- the variation substitutes a new reference object or context without declaring a new claim;
- the variation changes the PMS operator type instead of a concrete occurrence or relation;
- the expected target response cannot be formulated.

The test must preserve the distinction between feature and relation. A component can remain present while its ordering, access, dependency, or binding relation changes materially. Conversely, removing one visible component may be irrelevant where another source-supported structure carries the same bounded function.

```text
same elements
+ changed constitutive relation
→ possible relevant modification

removed element
+ preserved load-bearing relation through alternative support
→ possible weaker or no target change
```

#### C46-CONSTITUTIVEVARIATION-01 — Structural modification is distinct from semantic or incidental change

A constitutive variation changes one of the relations by which the source object carries the declared target. Examples include:

- changing the order of two path-forming occurrences where sequence is part of the composite claim;
- removing a component that the decomposition treats as necessary for the coarser function;
- changing an access or asymmetry relation that carries a projected coordination function;
- replacing a repeated Non-Event with an explicit decision where the trajectory claim depends on deferred commitment;
- changing the temporal window within which a branch was genuinely available;
- removing the specific source relation used to warrant a target function.

By contrast, the following are not sufficient by themselves:

- renaming a source feature;
- substituting a near-synonym;
- changing presentational granularity without changing the tested relation;
- changing an irrelevant descriptive attribute;
- replacing one operator label with another while leaving the source structure unexamined;
- imagining an unconstrained alternative world.

```text
label substitution
≠ structural modification

analogy variation
≠ source-grounded counterfactual
```

The PMS boundary is exact. Counterfactual variation may target concrete operator-typed occurrences or their source-supported relations. It may ask, for example, whether a projected function would persist if one documented Asymmetry occurrence were absent or if a Frame occurrence had a different supported boundary. It may not alter the Δ–Ψ operator type, rename an operator, reorder the canonical dependencies, decompose an operator type, or invent a new primitive.

```text
variation of operator occurrence or occurrence relation
→ potentially admissible

variation of operator type or Δ–Ψ dependency
→ prohibited
```

#### C46-EXPECTEDCHANGE-01 — The expected target response and claim effect must be declared

For every relevant modification, the analyst must state:

```yaml
expected_change:
  modified_source_feature:
  modification:
  preserved_source_conditions:
  expected_target_element_change:
  expected_claim_effect:
  alternative_expected_responses:
  uncertainty:
```

The expected target change may concern existence, boundary, order, intensity, distribution, duration, target function, Claim Scope, or the admissibility status of the claim. The declaration must be specific enough that an unchanged target, a partially changed target, and an unavailable response would be distinguishable.

Weak formulations are insufficient:

```text
“the result would somehow differ”
“the analysis would be less convincing”
“the operator would matter less”
```

A bounded formulation instead states, for example:

```text
If documented ordering relation R were reversed
while the remaining source conditions stayed fixed,
the trajectory claim Q would lose its asserted turning-point sequence
and would require reduction to a non-directional path claim.
```

This remains an expected response, not an empirical finding. Later sections determine whether sources support the variation, whether multiple responses remain plausible, and which sensitivity value is warranted.

#### C46-SOURCEDISCIPLINE-01 — Counterfactual pressure may not exceed source knowledge

The test must remain within three ceilings:

1. **Source Ceiling** — the available material must support the baseline feature, the proposed modification form, and the response space strongly enough for the stated claim.
2. **Claim Ceiling** — the counterfactual inference may not be broader than the source relation and target scope warrant.
3. **Authority Ceiling** — no sensitivity finding grants empirical, causal, normative, diagnostic, governance, or application authority.

```text
source supports F
≠ source supports every variation of F

source supports M(F)
≠ source proves actual alternate outcome

sensitivity finding
≠ causal or prescriptive authority
```

Where the sources support the baseline but not the proposed modification, the correct local state may later be `untestable`. Where the modification is supported but several target responses remain plausible, it may be `underdetermined`. Neither condition licenses free completion of the missing response.

The analyst must also disclose preserved background conditions. Without that declaration, an apparent response may result from changing the entire object rather than pressuring one load-bearing relation.

```text
bounded modification of F
+ declared preserved conditions
→ interpretable pressure

unbounded rewrite of S
→ no controlled sensitivity test
```

#### C46-WP1-HANDOFF-01 — WP1 hands WP2 a complete baseline and modification architecture without selecting a result

WP1 establishes:

- Counterfactual Sensitivity as a supplementary source-dependence load test;
- the core question linking relevant source change to target change;
- an explicit source baseline and analytical-coordinate requirement;
- admissible modification targets limited to constitutive, strongly modulating, temporally load-bearing, or functionally necessary features;
- the distinction between structural variation and arbitrary, semantic, incidental, or unsupported variation;
- a declared expected target response and claim effect;
- source, claim, PMS, causality, and authority discipline.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
C0:
  status: causality_assertion_not_accepted

F:
  result: unadjudicated

M:
  result: unadjudicated

R:
  result: unadjudicated

K:
  sensitivity_class: unselected

U:
  result: unadjudicated

counterfactual_test:
  route_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
```

WP1 selects no sensitivity value, pressure-object result, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, or canonical Output Class. It creates no Case, Test, or Smoke YAML. It adds no operation, Rule, audit stage, Loss field, schema field, PMS operator, dependency, person taxonomy, application route, or authority mechanism.

WP2 receives the baseline, relevant-modification, expected-response, and source-discipline architecture. It must specify the distinct `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS` test forms, fix the six-value vocabulary, distinguish strong, partial, weak, and insensitive findings, and preserve multiple plausible responses without adjudicating `F`, `M`, `R`, `K`, or `U`.

<a id="46-4-operation-specific-tests"></a>

### 46.4 Operation-Specific Tests

Counterfactual Sensitivity is common across STRATA, but the feature varied, the target response assessed, and the failure risk depend on the operation occurrence. A common test therefore does not imply one interchangeable test form. Each operation retains its canonical source, transformation direction, target, continuity burden, and Loss profile.

```text
one system-wide sensitivity principle
+ three operation-specific pressure forms
≠ one generic transformation
```

The operation must be classified before the modification is designed. Otherwise the analyst can vary a composite as though it were a projection, vary a projected function as though it were an origin type, or vary a decomposition result without preserving the same reference object. Such a test would pressure a different claim from the one declared.

#### C46-OPERATIONVARIANTS-01 — Each operation receives a distinct modification form without signature change

For `COMPOSE`, the relevant variation pressures selection, order, branching, event/non-event structure, or a constitutive relation among source structures. The question is whether the composite analytical object, its warranted relation structure, or its bounded claim changes when one declared load-bearing compositional condition changes.

```text
COMPOSE baseline:
selected source structures S1 ... Sn
+ declared order or relation R
→ composite object C

COMPOSE pressure:
source-bounded modification M(R, Si, branch, event, non-event)
→ expected change ΔC or ΔQ
```

The test does not ask whether any different collection could produce a different composite. It asks whether a change to a condition already claimed to carry this composite would alter the result. A changed source inventory with no preserved reference or selection rule is a new composition claim, not a controlled modification of the prior one.

For `DECOMPOSE`, the relevant variation changes or removes a component or relation within a source occurrence or composite while preserving the declared reference object and the question under which finer reconstruction was sought. The expected response may concern the resulting substructure, relation map, source-function return, Resolution Gain, Claim Scope, or Stop condition.

```text
DECOMPOSE baseline:
source occurrence or composite X
+ finer reconstruction D(X)
+ source-function return

DECOMPOSE pressure:
modify or remove component/relation F
while preserving reference X
→ expected change ΔD, Δreturn, or ΔQ
```

The test may not modify an operator type. Where a concrete PMS operator-typed occurrence is part of the source, only the occurrence or its source-supported relation may be varied. The Δ–Ψ operator, its name, dependency position, and canonical type remain unchanged.

For `PROJECT_AS`, the relevant variation pressures the origin-typed source feature or relation declared to carry the bounded target function. The question is whether the contextual function, its boundary, intensity, distribution, duration, or validity scope changes when that source load changes.

```text
PROJECT_AS baseline:
origin-typed source X
+ constitutive source feature F
+ target context C
→ bounded target function Y

PROJECT_AS pressure:
source-bounded modification M(F)
→ expected change ΔY, Δscope, or ΔQ
```

The source object retains its origin type throughout. A test that changes the target label while leaving the source relation unexamined is not Counterfactual Sensitivity. A test that changes the origin type to secure a target response is an invalid type jump, not a projection test.

The three forms therefore share one burden while preserving three signatures:

```text
COMPOSE
→ does the composite depend on declared selection, order, branch, event, non-event, or relation load?

DECOMPOSE
→ does the finer reconstruction and source-function return depend on the declared component or relation load?

PROJECT_AS
→ does the bounded target function depend on the declared origin-side feature or relation?
```

None of these forms adds a fourth operation, changes the Shared Transformation Record, or establishes a causal law. They specify where controlled pressure must be applied to the claim already made.

#### C46-OPERATIONEXAMPLES-01 — Bounded test forms expose operation-specific dependence without creating case packages

A bounded `COMPOSE` form may state:

```text
Baseline:
The composite claim Q depends on documented order R
between source occurrences A and B.

Modification:
Reverse R within a source-supported alternative sequence
while preserving the selected occurrences and Frame.

Expected response:
Q loses the claimed directional path relation
and must be reduced to a non-directional composite claim.
```

A bounded `DECOMPOSE` form may state:

```text
Baseline:
The finer reconstruction Q depends on relation R
linking components a and b to the source function of X.

Modification:
Remove R while preserving the same reference object X
and the remaining supported components.

Expected response:
The component list remains describable,
but the source-function return fails
and Q no longer qualifies as the same-reference decomposition claimed.
```

A bounded `PROJECT_AS` form may state:

```text
Baseline:
The projection Q treats source-side access relation R
as constitutive for bounded gatekeeping function Y in context C.

Modification:
Replace R with a source-supported symmetric-access relation
while preserving origin type, reference object, and context C.

Expected response:
Y narrows or disappears;
if the target label remains unchanged,
the projection requires an alternate source-load explanation.
```

These forms are method examples, not instantiated cases. They select no route, sensitivity value, Stop, Claim Reduction, or canonical Output Class.

An unchanged-label failure form is equally necessary:

```text
materially opposed constitutive source configurations S1 and S2
+ unchanged target label Y
+ no reconstructible invariant
+ no alternate source-load explanation
→ source-indifferent label persistence pressure
```

This form does not by itself prove that the label is elastic or the transformation failed. It identifies the burden that later interpretation must answer. The target may be warranted under a higher invariant, replaceable support, or a narrower function. Where no such explanation can be reconstructed, stable wording cannot count as evidence of stable structure.

<a id="46-5-sensitivity-classes"></a>

### 46.5 Sensitivity Classes

STRATA uses exactly six Counterfactual Sensitivity values:

```text
strongly_sensitive
partially_sensitive
weakly_sensitive
insensitive
underdetermined
untestable
```

#### C46-SENSITIVITYCLASSES-01 — The six-value vocabulary is closed and non-convertible

The values may not be renamed, expanded, split, collapsed, or supplemented by local quasi-classes. They are not canonical Output Classes, operation results, confidence scores, causal grades, or authority levels.

```text
sensitivity value
≠ canonical Output Class
≠ probability score
≠ causal strength
≠ claim authority
```

The vocabulary is also not a universal total order. `strongly_sensitive`, `partially_sensitive`, and `weakly_sensitive` distinguish claim-relative dependence where a target response can be bounded. `insensitive` records a sufficiently tested absence of the expected response under the declared modification. `underdetermined` and `untestable` identify different limits on classification. The latter two cannot be ranked as weaker forms of insensitivity.

Every value must be attached to a declared feature, modification, target element, Frame, temporal scope, and Claim Scope. A record may therefore contain more than one sensitivity finding where distinct target elements or source features respond differently. Such local findings must not be compressed into a single global label unless the aggregation rule and Loss are independently warranted.

```text
Q sensitive to F1
+ Q insensitive to F2
≠ one global sensitivity value automatically
```

#### C46-STRONGPARTIALWEAK-01 — Strong, partial, and weak sensitivity are claim-relative dependence forms without numerical scoring

A result is `strongly_sensitive` where a source-supported modification of the declared load-bearing feature requires a material change to the central target claim, object, function, relation, or admissibility scope, and the sources discriminate that response from relevant alternatives.

```text
M(F)
→ material ΔQ at the claim core
+ alternatives sufficiently discriminated
→ candidate strongly_sensitive finding
```

“Material” does not mean large in a universal metric. It means that the prior claim cannot remain substantially intact under its declared scope. The response may require withdrawal, structural reformulation, operation reclassification, substantial Claim Reduction, or Stop. None follows mechanically from the sensitivity value alone.

A result is `partially_sensitive` where the modification changes a bounded part, condition, range, temporal segment, distribution, or target-function scope while a specified residual claim remains supported.

```text
M(F)
→ ΔQ_part
+ residual Q_bounded remains supported
→ candidate partially_sensitive finding
```

Partial sensitivity must name both the changed and retained portions. It cannot function as vague middle language used when the analysis has not declared the target response precisely. A partial result may support a bounded claim, but it does not automatically map to `admissible_with_bounded_claim`, `partially_admissible`, or `claim_reduction_required`.

A result is `weakly_sensitive` where a warranted modification produces a detectable but limited target response that does not materially alter the central claim under the declared scope, yet is not analytically negligible. The response may concern a secondary boundary, intensity, timing, distribution, or uncertainty condition.

```text
M(F)
→ detectable ΔQ_secondary
+ central Q remains substantially stable
→ candidate weakly_sensitive finding
```

Weak sensitivity is not “almost insensitive,” and it is not a lower-quality finding. The relevant question is what claim element changes and what remains. A small but load-bearing temporal shift can matter more for one claim than a broad descriptive change matters for another.

No numerical thresholds separate the three values. Number of changed fields, percentage of retained wording, effect size, source count, or operator-occurrence count cannot decide the class mechanically.

```text
more changed details
≠ stronger sensitivity automatically

more PMS operator occurrences affected
≠ stronger sensitivity automatically
```

The distinction remains qualitative, source-bounded, and claim-relative.

#### C46-MULTIRESPONSE-01 — Multiple plausible target responses preserve underdetermination rather than forcing one class

A relevant modification may support several materially different target responses. The analyst must preserve them where the source basis does not discriminate among them.

```text
M(F)
→ plausible ΔQ1
or plausible ΔQ2
+ sources do not discriminate
→ underdetermined
```

The correct response is not to select the analytically preferred outcome, average the alternatives, or label the result partially sensitive by default. `underdetermined` means that the modification can be formulated and relevant responses are identifiable, but the available warrant does not determine which response or sensitivity relation obtains.

This is distinct from `untestable`. An `untestable` result arises where the source basis does not support the modification, preserved conditions, response space, or comparison strongly enough to perform the test. WP3 will complete that source-status distinction and its claim effects.

Multiple response preservation also protects against hidden teleology. The target response may strengthen, weaken, narrow, split, disappear, remain stable for a warranted reason, or become unavailable. The method may not choose the response that best preserves the original theory.

<a id="46-6-insensitive-result"></a>

### 46.6 Insensitive Result

An `insensitive` finding records that a sufficiently supported, relevant modification did not produce the target response expected from the declared dependence under the stated preserved conditions and Claim Scope.

```text
relevant M(F)
+ adequate test conditions
+ target element Q remains materially unchanged
→ candidate insensitive finding
```

#### C46-INSENSITIVITY-01 — Insensitivity is interpretive pressure, not a self-interpreting verdict

Insensitivity may indicate at least four different structures:

1. **warranted robustness** — the target depends on a broader or alternative invariant preserved across the modification;
2. **replaceability** — the modified feature is load-bearing in the baseline but another supported feature carries the bounded target under the changed configuration;
3. **elasticity or source indifference** — the target label remains stable despite changes that should matter and no invariant or alternate load can be reconstructed;
4. **misspecification** — the feature, modification, target element, preserved conditions, or expected response was incorrectly declared.

```text
insensitive
≠ robust automatically
≠ elastic automatically
≠ failed_transformation automatically
≠ feature irrelevant automatically
```

The analyst must therefore return to the source trace. The relevant questions are:

- Was `F` actually constitutive for the tested target element?
- Was `M(F)` source-grounded and sufficiently discriminating?
- Were preserved background conditions declared correctly?
- Did an alternate source feature take over the load?
- Does a higher invariant explain target stability?
- Was the target claim too broad or too vague to register change?
- Did the test vary the source occurrence rather than merely its label?

Insensitivity can pressure the original claim strongly without settling its final disposition. A target that was claimed to depend specifically on `F` may require narrowing even where the broader target remains supportable through another relation. Conversely, a target may remain stable under several source variations because the claim correctly identifies an invariant.

#### C46-ROBUSTNESSBOUNDARY-01 — Stable target response counts as robustness only with a reconstructible invariant or alternate source-load explanation

A robustness interpretation requires more than survival of the target label. The record must identify either:

- an invariant relation genuinely preserved across the baseline and modified source configurations; or
- an alternate source-supported load path that carries the same bounded target function under the modification.

```text
unchanged Q under M(F)
+ reconstructible invariant I
or alternate load path A
+ bounded validity scope
→ possible warranted robustness interpretation
```

The invariant or alternate load must itself remain traceable, type-integral, context-bounded, and sensitive to possible defeaters. It may not be introduced solely after the original feature fails the test without being registered as a new or revised claim.

```text
post hoc alternate explanation
without source trace
≠ robustness warrant
```

Where opposite constitutive source structures yield the same target label and neither an invariant nor alternate load can be reconstructed, the finding pressures macro-label elasticity or projection-by-resemblance. Chapter 45 supplies the Traceability-Ceiling burden; Chapter 50 later checks whether a new invariant is being used to immunize the prior claim. Chapter 46 does not itself select the final canonical Output Class.

Robustness also remains bounded to the tested modification range. Stability under one variation does not establish stability under all relevant source changes.

```text
Q stable under M1(F)
≠ Q stable under every M(F)
≠ causal law
```

The result carries no normative, diagnostic, governance, application, or action authority.

#### C46-WP2-HANDOFF-01 — WP2 hands WP3 the operation forms and closed sensitivity architecture without adjudicating the pressure packet

WP2 establishes:

- distinct `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS` modification forms without operation redefinition;
- one bounded test form for each operation and one unchanged-label failure form;
- the exact closed six-value sensitivity vocabulary;
- claim-relative distinctions among strong, partial, and weak sensitivity without numerical scoring;
- preservation of multiple plausible target responses through `underdetermined` where sources do not discriminate;
- insensitivity as interpretive pressure rather than automatic robustness, elasticity, irrelevance, or failure;
- a reconstructible invariant or alternate source-load requirement for warranted robustness.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
C0:
  status: causality_assertion_not_accepted

F:
  result: unadjudicated

M:
  result: unadjudicated

R:
  result: unadjudicated

K:
  sensitivity_class: unselected

U:
  result: unadjudicated

counterfactual_test:
  route_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
```

No sensitivity value is assigned to the packet. No pressure-object result, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, or canonical Output Class is selected. No Case, Test, or Smoke YAML is created.

WP3 receives the operation-specific forms, closed six-value vocabulary, multiple-response rule, and insensitivity interpretation burden. It must distinguish source-grounded counterfactuals, bounded hypothetical variations, speculative scenarios, `underdetermined`, and `untestable`; define the controlled Shared-Record view and claim effects; preserve prior dispositions across re-entry; and hand the completed method to Chapters 47–53 without adjudicating `F`, `M`, `R`, `K`, or `U`.

<a id="46-7-counterfactual-source-discipline"></a>

### 46.7 Counterfactual Source Discipline

Counterfactual Sensitivity is useful only where the proposed variation remains anchored to the declared source object, the available source basis, and the transformation claim under pressure. The method therefore distinguishes four forms that must not be collapsed:

1. **source-grounded counterfactual** — both the relevant feature and the proposed modification are supported by the source basis or by a tightly warranted contrast already represented within it;
2. **bounded hypothetical variation** — the modification is not directly observed but is constrained by supported ranges, relations, alternatives, or historically available configurations;
3. **speculative scenario** — the variation is imaginable but lacks sufficient source support, preserved conditions, or a traceable relation to the target claim;
4. **unavailable counterfactual** — the present source basis does not permit a defensible modification or target-response test at all.

```text
source-grounded counterfactual
≠ bounded hypothetical variation
≠ speculative scenario
≠ unavailable counterfactual
```

The distinctions concern warrant, not literary vividness. A detailed scenario can remain speculative, while a compact variation may be source-grounded if its load-bearing feature, admissible range, preserved background conditions, and expected target response are reconstructible.

A source-grounded counterfactual may rely on documented variation across occurrences, periods, roles, branches, sequences, or comparable arrangements. A bounded hypothetical variation may be permissible where the source does not instantiate the exact modification but supports the relevant structural range. For example, a source may document several admissible orderings, partial removals, delayed transitions, or alternative role distributions even if one exact configuration was not observed. The analyst may pressure the claim within that supported range, but may not narrate a determinate empirical outcome that the sources do not establish.

```text
supported variation range
→ possible bounded hypothetical test

supported variation range
≠ observed alternate outcome
≠ causal proof
```

A speculative scenario lacks one or more constitutive controls. It may change several source features at once, leave background conditions undeclared, import an unsupported mechanism, alter the reference object, or assume a target response solely because it is theoretically convenient. Such a scenario may be retained as an explicit analogy, exploratory prompt, or future evidence requirement, but it cannot carry a Counterfactual Sensitivity class as though it were a completed source-grounded test.

An unavailable counterfactual is stronger than a mere warning about speculation. It records that the necessary test object cannot presently be constructed. The source basis may not support the baseline, the relevant modification, the preserved conditions, or the response space. The correct result is therefore not an invented variation but an explicit limit.

#### C46-COUNTERFACTUALSOURCEDISCIPLINE-01 — Counterfactual forms are separated by source support and boundedness

The minimum classification burden is:

```yaml
counterfactual_source_status:
  baseline_supported:
  feature_supported:
  modification_supported:
  preserved_conditions_supported:
  response_space_supported:
  source_status:
```

`source_status` may describe the proposed test as source-grounded, bounded hypothetical, speculative, or unavailable. This is a controlled local description, not a new canonical Output Class and not an additional value in the six-member Counterfactual Sensitivity vocabulary.

A bounded hypothetical variation must state why the modification remains within the source-supported structural range. Acceptable grounds may include:

- documented variation across comparable occurrences;
- an explicitly represented alternative branch or sequence;
- a source-supported interval of duration, intensity, participation, or recurrence;
- a relation whose removal, reversal, or substitution is already evidenced as possible;
- a documented but unrealized option, commitment, or Non-Event;
- a contrastive source that preserves the reference object and relevant context.

The analyst must also identify what remains fixed. A proposed modification that silently changes Frame, reference object, granularity, temporal scope, target function, or Claim Scope is not one controlled counterfactual. It is a new transformation claim or a bundle of claims and must be recorded as such.

```text
change in F under preserved C
≠ change in F + Frame + object + target function
```

The PMS boundary remains exact. A counterfactual may vary a concrete, source-supported operator occurrence or relation where that occurrence is claim-bearing. It may not vary the Δ–Ψ operator type, dependency order, operator layer, name, primitive status, or the grammar itself.

```text
source-supported occurrence variation
→ possible test input

operator-type or dependency variation
→ prohibited test input
```

Where a proposed variation requires an invented operator occurrence, a newly defined operator type, or a reordered Δ–Ψ dependency merely to preserve the target claim, the method must register unsupported construction or possible analytical self-immunization rather than treating the variation as evidence.

Counterfactual source discipline also preserves prior dispositions. A new source or a newly supported modification may justify re-entry, but it creates a new testable claim. It does not retroactively convert an earlier `untestable`, Stop, Claim Reduction, failure, or Non-Capture result into success.

```text
new source-supported variation
= new testable claim

new testable claim
≠ prior disposition erased
```

<a id="46-8-untestable"></a>

### 46.8 Untestable

`untestable` applies where the current source basis does not support the counterfactual test strongly enough to evaluate the declared dependence. The obstacle may concern the baseline, the feature, the modification, preserved background conditions, the target-response space, or the comparison required to interpret the result.

```text
relevant dependence claim stated
+ counterfactual test cannot be source-bounded
→ untestable
```

The value is substantive because it identifies a specific evidential and methodological limit. It is not a placeholder for analyst uncertainty in general, and it must name what prevents the test.

#### C46-UNTESTABLE-01 — Untestable records unavailable testing without truth, falsity, robustness, or immunity inference

Typical grounds include:

- the source does not identify whether `F` is present or load-bearing in the baseline;
- the source does not support a relevant modification of `F`;
- preserved conditions cannot be specified without changing the reference object or context;
- the target response cannot be reconstructed at the necessary granularity;
- irrecoverable Loss removed the relation needed for comparison;
- the available evidence contains only the target label and not the source structure;
- the proposed test would require an empirical experiment or alternate history unavailable to the analysis;
- relevant occurrences or relations cannot be distinguished without exceeding the Source Ceiling.

The record must state the blocking condition:

```yaml
untestable_basis:
  missing_baseline:
  missing_feature_warrant:
  missing_modification_warrant:
  missing_preserved_conditions:
  missing_response_space:
  irrecoverable_loss:
  source_ceiling:
```

This list is a conceptual view of existing source, uncertainty, Loss, and Claim-Ceiling information. It is not a required new schema object.

The following non-equivalences are mandatory:

```text
untestable
≠ true
≠ false
≠ insensitive
≠ robust
≠ elastic
≠ failed_transformation automatically
≠ immunity from further scrutiny
```

An untestable dependence claim may remain provisionally stated only within a correspondingly reduced Claim Scope and only if other applicable gates permit that bounded retention. It may instead require Claim Reduction, Mandatory Stop, `non_capture`, or another canonical disposition after the complete audit. Chapter 46 does not decide that mapping mechanically.

`untestable` cannot protect an overclaim. The absence of a test does not authorize the strongest formulation. Where the target claim expressly asserts dependence on `F`, but the relation cannot be tested or traced, the method must reduce confidence, scope, or asserted necessity rather than treating unavailable testing as corroboration.

```text
cannot test dependence
≠ dependence presumed

cannot falsify Q
≠ Q warranted
```

Re-entry remains possible where a new source, newly recoverable relation, better-preserved occurrence, or revised bounded question makes the test constructible. The re-entry record must preserve the earlier untestable result and identify the exact new condition that changes testability.

<a id="46-9-underdetermined-and-claim-effects"></a>

### 46.9 Underdetermined and Claim Effects

`underdetermined` applies where the counterfactual test can be constructed, but the available source basis supports more than one materially different target response or sensitivity interpretation and does not discriminate among them.

```text
baseline supported
+ relevant modification supported
+ multiple materially different responses remain plausible
+ sources do not discriminate
→ underdetermined
```

The test is therefore available, unlike an `untestable` case. What remains unavailable is a warranted selection among the supported response paths.

#### C46-UNDERDETERMINED-01 — Underdetermined preserves multiple testable responses and remains distinct from unavailable testing

The distinction is exact:

```text
untestable
→ test cannot be adequately constructed

underdetermined
→ test can be constructed,
  but result cannot be uniquely warranted
```

An underdetermined record must identify the live alternatives. It is insufficient to write that “the result is unclear.” The analyst must state the materially different responses, the evidence supporting each, the missing discriminator, and the effect on the target claim.

```yaml
underdetermined_response_space:
  modification:
  response_candidates:
  support_for_each:
  missing_discriminator:
  claim_effect:
```

The response candidates may differ in direction, scope, timing, function, or disposition. One may imply strong sensitivity, another partial sensitivity, and another warranted stability. The existence of multiple candidates does not justify averaging them, selecting the theoretically preferred response, or assigning `partially_sensitive` as a compromise value.

```text
strongly_sensitive plausible
+ insensitive plausible
+ no discriminator
→ underdetermined

not
→ partially_sensitive by averaging
```

Underdetermination may be local. One target element can be strongly sensitive while another remains underdetermined. One temporal interval may support a bounded response while a later interval does not. The record must avoid collapsing heterogeneous local findings into one global sensitivity value unless an integrated claim and warrant are separately established.

#### C46-FAILURECLAIMEFFECT-01 — Local sensitivity results affect claims only through bounded, audited mappings

A sensitivity value is a local methodological finding. It does not mechanically select a canonical Output Class. Its claim effect depends on what the original claim asserted, which source feature was declared load-bearing, what changed or remained stable, and which other admissibility gates pass.

Possible claim effects include:

- retention of a bounded claim where sensitivity matches the declared dependence;
- narrowing of scope where only part of the target responds;
- separation of one claim into multiple claims with different source relations;
- removal of a necessity, universality, or exclusivity assertion;
- downgrade from structural projection to `analogy_only` where resemblance remains but dependence does not;
- Claim Reduction where a residual traceable claim survives;
- Mandatory Stop where the current route cannot carry its declared dependence;
- Non-Capture where the relevant relation cannot be represented or tested without distortion;
- preservation of uncertainty where the result is underdetermined or untestable.

These are possible routes, not automatic mappings.

```text
strongly_sensitive
≠ admissible automatically

partially_sensitive
≠ partially_admissible automatically

weakly_sensitive
≠ admissible_with_bounded_claim automatically

insensitive
≠ failed_transformation automatically

underdetermined
≠ non_capture automatically

untestable
≠ mandatory_stop automatically
```

The complete mapping must consider at least PraxisPurchase, TraceableLoad, TypeIntegrity, Reference and Functional Continuity, Temporal Continuity where relevant, Contextual Boundedness, alternatives, five-part Loss, Source Ceiling, Claim Ceiling, Stop conditions, and Non-Capture.

Weak or partial sensitivity cannot be used as rhetorical insulation. If the original claim requires strong dependence, a weak response may materially defeat or reduce it. Conversely, a weak response may be entirely adequate for a claim that asserted only limited modulation. The claim effect is therefore relative to the declared burden, not to an assumed hierarchy of sensitivity values.

```text
observed sensitivity
compared with
claimed dependence burden
→ bounded claim effect
```

Prior dispositions remain visible across reformulation. If `Q_strong` fails its declared sensitivity burden and `Q_bounded` is then introduced, the bounded claim is a new testable claim. Its later success cannot erase the earlier failure or reduction.

<a id="46-10-shared-record-view-limits-and-handoff"></a>

### 46.10 Shared-Record View, Limits, and Handoff

Counterfactual Sensitivity is recorded through a controlled view of the Shared Transformation Record. It does not introduce a new top-level record, operation, schema field, audit stage, Output Class, or PMS primitive.

#### C46-RECORDVIEW-01 — The Counterfactual Sensitivity view maps to existing Shared-Record fields

The view draws on existing declarations for:

- reference object and transformation occurrence;
- operation type;
- source baseline and Source Ceiling;
- Frame, context, granularities, relative level, and temporal scope;
- declared load-bearing feature or relation;
- Constitutive Source Trace;
- relevant source modification and preserved conditions;
- target claim, expected response, and Claim Scope;
- alternatives, uncertainty, and five-part Loss;
- local sensitivity value where warranted;
- claim effect and later canonical routing;
- Stop, Claim Reduction, re-entry, and Non-Capture where subsequently adjudicated.

A conceptual rendering may use:

```yaml
counterfactual_sensitivity_view:
  baseline:
  load_bearing_feature:
  source_status:
  relevant_modification:
  preserved_conditions:
  target_claim:
  expected_response:
  observed_or_warranted_response:
  sensitivity_value:
  alternatives:
  uncertainty:
  claim_effect:
```

This rendering is neither a new schema requirement nor proof that the fields contain a substantively warranted test.

```text
schema-valid Counterfactual Sensitivity view
≠ source-grounded variation
≠ warranted response
≠ causal proof
≠ admissibility result
```

The Formal Model may verify that the sensitivity value belongs to the six-member vocabulary, that required references exist, that the operation inventory remains closed, and that local findings are not serialized as new Output Classes. It may not decide which source feature is constitutive, which modification is relevant, which target response would occur, whether a causal relation exists, or which canonical disposition is substantively correct.

#### C46-NOCAUSALPROOF-01 — Counterfactual Sensitivity grants neither causal proof nor authority

The method has four explicit limits:

1. it is not an empirical intervention or experiment;
2. it does not reconstruct an actual alternate history;
3. it does not prove causal necessity, sufficiency, or universal law;
4. it does not grant normative, diagnostic, governance, application, recommendation, or action authority.

```text
source-bounded dependence pressure
≠ causal identification
≠ empirical outcome proof
≠ complete validation
≠ authority grant
```

A strong sensitivity finding may support the internal dependence claim of a transformation. It does not establish that the varied feature is the sole cause, that the target would change identically in every context, or that intervention on the feature is feasible, legitimate, or advisable.

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

#### C46-HANDOFF47-53-01 — Later LIMITS chapters retain their independent burdens

Chapter 46 hands forward a completed Counterfactual Sensitivity method, not a completed transformation audit.

```text
Chapter 47
→ Reference, Functional, Temporal, and TypeIntegrity-view continuity

Chapter 48
→ selection, compression, exclusion, uncertainty, and irrecoverable Loss

Chapter 49
→ Source Ceiling, Claim Ceiling, and calibration boundary

Chapter 50
→ Anti-Immunization across successor claims and explanations

Chapter 51
→ integrated Stop, Claim Reduction, and re-entry

Chapter 52
→ Non-Capture

Chapter 53
→ integrated twelve-stage audit
```

Chapter 46 does not pre-empt any of these owners. A sensitivity result may generate pressure relevant to them, but each later burden requires its own record-level test.

#### C46-HANDOFF-01 — WP3 hands WP4 a complete method without adjudicating the synthetic pressure packet

Chapter 46 now establishes:

- Counterfactual Sensitivity as a source-dependence load test rather than causal proof;
- the exact core question, baseline, relevant-modification, preserved-condition, expected-response, and source-discipline burdens;
- distinct `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS` modification forms without operation redefinition;
- exactly six sensitivity values with no numerical scoring or automatic Output mapping;
- strong, partial, weak, insensitive, underdetermined, and untestable results as bounded local findings;
- source-grounded, bounded hypothetical, speculative, and unavailable counterfactual forms;
- the exact distinction between `underdetermined` and `untestable`;
- bounded claim effects without mechanical routing;
- Shared-Record mapping without a second grammar;
- explicit causal, empirical, formal-model, PMS, and authority limits;
- handoff to Chapters 47–53.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
C0:
  status: causality_assertion_not_accepted

F:
  result: unadjudicated

M:
  result: unadjudicated

R:
  result: unadjudicated

K:
  sensitivity_class: unselected

U:
  result: unadjudicated

counterfactual_test:
  route_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
```

No sensitivity value, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, canonical Output Class, Case, Test, or Smoke artifact is selected or created. WP4 must audit the full Chapter-46 Contract, all twenty-four duties, the exact six-value vocabulary, the pressure packet, PMS and record integrity, redundancy, ownership, and authority boundaries; determine the bounded chapter-level lock; and prepare Chapter 47 without allowing a chapter-level result to flow to `F`, `M`, `R`, `K`, or `U`.

## 47. Reference, Type, and Function Continuity

<a id="47-1-continuity-dimensions-and-type-integrity-view"></a>

### 47.1 Continuity Dimensions and Type-Integrity View

A STRATA transformation may preserve one dimension of continuity while weakening, narrowing, or breaking another. Continuity therefore cannot be represented as one undifferentiated yes-or-no property of a transformation result. Chapter 47 separates the dimensions that must remain visible whenever a source object is opened, combined, or assigned a bounded target function.

The three continuity dimensions are:

1. **Reference Continuity** — whether the same historical or structural referent remains identifiable through the transformation;
2. **Functional Continuity** — whether the claimed bounded function remains dependent on concrete source features in the declared context;
3. **Temporal Continuity**, where claim-bearing — whether relevant order, duration, sedimentation, turning points, Non-Events, irreversibility, and historical load remain sufficiently reconstructible.

Typing is audited alongside these dimensions, but it does not become a fourth continuity Rule. The phrase `type continuity` is retained only as continuity-language for the preservation or warranted revision of typing under the canonical `TypeIntegrity` Rule.

```text
type continuity
=
continuity view under canonical TypeIntegrity

not

new Rule
new primitive
fourth independent continuity check
```

This distinction protects the closed Rule inventory. Chapter 47 may ask whether an origin type remains visible, whether a component has been illicitly retyped through a composite, or whether a target function has overwritten the source type. It does not create a seventeenth Rule or a second type system.

#### C47-DIMENSIONS-01 — Reference, Functional, and Temporal Continuity are distinct declarations

Each applicable dimension must be declared and tested separately. A record that states only `continuity: preserved` conceals which burden has actually been met.

A minimally adequate continuity declaration therefore distinguishes:

```yaml
continuity_view:
  reference:
    status:
    basis:
    limits:
  type_integrity_view:
    status:
    basis:
    limits:
  function:
    status:
    basis:
    limits:
  temporal:
    applicable:
    status:
    basis:
    limits:
```

This is a conceptual view of existing Shared Transformation Record fields. It does not introduce a new top-level record or schema requirement. The view makes dimension-specific reasoning visible; substantive warrant must still come from the source, the operation record, the declared coordinates, and the applicable audit rules.

The dimensions answer different questions:

```text
same referent?
≠ same typing burden?
≠ same bounded function?
≠ same temporal formation?
```

A transformation may preserve the referent while losing the function. It may preserve origin typing while narrowing the target function. It may retain the reference object while temporal compression removes the historical sequence needed for a particular claim. None of these combinations is contradictory merely because the local findings differ.

#### C47-TYPEINTEGRITYVIEW-01 — Type continuity remains subordinate to canonical TypeIntegrity

The TypeIntegrity view asks whether the transformation respects the existing distinctions among:

- operator type;
- operator occurrence;
- composite analytical object;
- origin type;
- target function;
- derived object or profile;
- component type and composite type.

Its governing boundary is:

```text
origin type
≠ target function

component type
≠ composite type automatically

derived profile
≠ new PMS primitive
```

For `PROJECT_AS`, the source object retains its origin type while performing a bounded target function. For `COMPOSE`, the new composite analytical object does not retroactively rewrite the types of its components. For `DECOMPOSE`, only an occurrence or composite is opened; the operator type itself is not decomposed.

These are applications of `TypeIntegrity`. Chapter 47 does not redefine the Rule, and WP1 does not yet operationalize the full operation-specific type procedure owned by §§47.4–47.5. It fixes only the architectural status of the type-continuity language so later continuity findings cannot silently create a new Rule.

The formal implication is limited:

```text
TypeIntegrity-view preserved
≠ Reference Continuity proved
≠ Functional Continuity proved
≠ Temporal Continuity proved
≠ complete admissibility proved
```

Likewise, a type problem does not by itself determine one universal canonical Output Class. Its claim effect depends on the affected operation, the retained reference, the remaining source trace, the declared function, and the complete audit.

#### C47-DIMENSIONSEPARATION-01 — Mixed local continuity findings remain visible until integrated audit

Continuity findings must not be collapsed prematurely. A possible local matrix may read:

```yaml
local_continuity_findings:
  reference: preserved
  type_integrity_view: preserved_with_limit
  function: uncertain
  temporal: partial
  chain_result: unselected
  output_class: unselected
```

This matrix does not decide whether the transformation is admissible, provisional, reducible, analogous, stopped, failed, or uncaptured. It preserves the structure needed for a later integrated decision.

The method therefore rejects two opposite errors:

1. **binary collapse** — one passed dimension is treated as total continuity;
2. **failure contagion** — one local discontinuity is treated as automatic failure of every dimension and every possible bounded claim.

```text
one local pass
≠ total continuity

one local failure
≠ all dimensions fail automatically
```

Mixed findings can be methodologically decisive. If reference remains stable but function becomes source-independent, the target function may fail while the source-object reconstruction remains usable. If type and function remain stable but relevant temporal formation is uncertain, a temporally broad claim may require narrowing without invalidating every local statement. The exact mapping belongs to WP3 and later audit chapters.

The synthetic continuity pressure packet remains untouched:

```yaml
N0:
  status: nominal_sameness_assertion_not_accepted
CR:
  result: unadjudicated
CT:
  result: unadjudicated
CF:
  result: unadjudicated
CTM:
  result: unadjudicated
CC:
  result: unadjudicated
```

No local or chain continuity result, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, or canonical Output Class is selected in WP1.

<a id="47-2-reference-continuity"></a>

### 47.2 Reference Continuity

Reference Continuity asks whether a transformation continues to concern the same historical or structural object. The burden is not satisfied by repeating the source name, preserving a label, or retaining a superficial description. The referent must remain identifiable through those constitutive relations that make it the object claimed by the transformation.

Reference Continuity is therefore relational and claim-bound:

```text
reference object X
+ historical or structural formation H
+ constitutive relation bundle R
+ preservation criterion P
+ transformation occurrence T
→ test whether X remains the referent of the target claim
```

The object need not remain unchanged in every detail. `DECOMPOSE` opens it into finer structures; `COMPOSE` may place it within a new analytical composite; `PROJECT_AS` may assign it a bounded target function. Continuity concerns continued identifiability through transformation, not static identity or losslessness.

#### C47-REFERENCECONTINUITY-01 — The same referent remains identifiable through preserved constitutive relations

A warranted Reference Continuity claim should identify at least:

- the source reference object;
- the historical process or structural formation through which it exists;
- the constitutive relations that distinguish it from relevant alternatives;
- the transformation occurrence applied to it;
- the preservation criterion under which the target still concerns that object;
- the declared limits and Loss affecting identifiability.

The test is not merely lexical. It asks whether the source object's identity-bearing relations remain reconstructible. Depending on the object and claim, these relations may include:

- role and dependency relations;
- ordering and succession;
- recurrent practices;
- institutional commitments;
- boundaries of inclusion and exclusion;
- cost and exposure distributions;
- event and Non-Event relations;
- path-forming constraints;
- source-function relations;
- operator-occurrence relations where PMS typing is claim-bearing.

A compressed or projected target may omit many details and still preserve reference if the identity-bearing relation bundle remains recoverable and the omissions are declared. Conversely, a target may reproduce many surface features while replacing the relation bundle that constituted the source object.

```text
many shared attributes
≠ same referent automatically

substantial declared compression
+ preserved constitutive relation bundle
→ possible Reference Continuity
```

Reference Continuity is compatible with change. A historical institution, practice configuration, trajectory, or composite can change while remaining the same referent for a bounded claim, provided the record states which continuities and transformations sustain that identification. The method does not require metaphysical sameness or an immutable core. It requires a reconstructible warrant for continued reference.

This is especially important for temporal objects. A trajectory or path is not identified merely by a recurring label but by the ordered formation, constraints, turning points, and dependencies relevant to the claim. The complete Temporal Continuity test remains WP3, but reference identity cannot ignore historical formation where that formation constitutes the object.

#### C47-SAMEREFERENT-01 — The record must declare object, process, relation bundle, and preservation criterion

A continuity claim is incomplete unless it makes the same-referent burden inspectable. The minimum declaration is:

```yaml
same_referent_declaration:
  source_reference_object:
  source_historical_or_structural_process:
  constitutive_relation_bundle:
  relevant_alternatives:
  transformation_occurrence:
  preservation_criterion:
  retained_identifier_or_trace:
  declared_compression_or_loss:
  claim_scope:
```

The **preservation criterion** is essential. It states what must remain true for the target claim still to concern the source object. A preservation criterion may require continuity of a relation bundle, a documented institutional lineage, a source-function dependency, an ordered temporal formation, or another claim-bearing identifier. It cannot be reduced to `same name` or `analyst treats as same`.

The criterion must also be discriminating. If materially different source objects, histories, and relation bundles all satisfy it without explanation, the criterion is too elastic to carry Reference Continuity. That problem may interact with the Traceability Ceiling, Counterfactual Sensitivity, calibration, or Anti-Immunization, but WP1 records only the reference burden.

For PMS-typed material, the referent is a concrete occurrence, configuration, sequence, path, trajectory, or composite — not the abstract Δ–Ψ operator type. A stable operator label can aid navigation but cannot establish that two historical occurrences are the same referent.

```text
same operator type
≠ same operator occurrence
≠ same historical referent
```

Two distinct □-typed occurrences remain distinct occurrences even if their typing is identical. One occurrence may continue through change while another merely shares its type. Reference Continuity therefore requires occurrence-level and relation-level trace where PMS typing is relevant.

#### C47-NOMINALSAMENESS-01 — Names and labels do not prove Reference Continuity

Nominal sameness includes:

- the same institutional name;
- the same category label;
- the same operator label;
- the same target-function wording;
- the same variable or identifier;
- repeated reference by the same shorthand.
