These may be evidence that a continuity claim should be investigated. They are never sufficient proof.

```text
same name
≠ same historical process
≠ same constitutive relations
≠ same reference object automatically
```

The reverse is also true. A change of name does not by itself prove Reference Discontinuity. The historical or structural referent may remain continuous despite relabeling, translation, merger terminology, or revised analytical notation.

```text
different name
≠ different referent automatically
```

Nominal evidence is therefore subordinate to the declared process, relation bundle, and preservation criterion. This protects STRATA from both false continuity and false discontinuity produced by labels alone.

<a id="47-3-reference-discontinuity"></a>

### 47.3 Reference Discontinuity

Reference Discontinuity occurs where the target no longer concerns the source object asserted by the transformation, even though wording, labels, selected attributes, or analytical convenience may suggest continuity. It is a failure of continued reference, not merely a change in description.

Typical forms include:

- the source historical process is replaced by a different process;
- the constitutive relation bundle is exchanged while the name persists;
- the target selects a successor object but continues to speak as though it were the source object;
- a composite absorbs one component's name while excluding the relations that made the component the original referent;
- a decomposition produces fragments that cannot be returned to the same source object;
- a projection becomes attached to a generic target label while the originating object has ceased to carry the relevant source relation.

#### C47-REFERENCEDISCONTINUITY-01 — Nominal persistence may mask replacement of object, process, or relation bundle

A Reference Discontinuity finding requires more than noticing change. It must show that the transformation has crossed the declared preservation criterion. The test asks:

```text
Which identity-bearing relation or historical formation
was required for continued reference,
and where is it replaced, severed, or made unrecoverable?
```

Possible evidence includes:

- a documented break in institutional lineage;
- replacement of the relevant role or dependency structure;
- substitution of a new historical process under the old name;
- loss of the source-object return path after decomposition;
- a composite whose selection excludes the identity-bearing component relation;
- a projected function that remains verbally attached to the source name while its source object is no longer identifiable.

The finding remains claim-relative. A discontinuity for one broad historical claim may coexist with continuity for a narrower functional or administrative claim. The record must state which reference claim fails and which, if any, residual reference remains defensible.

```text
reference discontinuity for Q_strong
≠ no possible bounded reference claim
```

Likewise, continuity cannot be restored by switching frames, granularities, target functions, or labels without creating a new testable claim. A successor reconstruction may be warranted, but it does not erase the earlier discontinuity.

```text
new reference claim
= new testable claim

later bounded continuity
≠ earlier reference discontinuity erased
```

Reference Discontinuity also does not automatically decide TypeIntegrity, Functional Continuity, Temporal Continuity, or the final canonical Output Class. A target may retain a useful analogy after reference identity fails; a narrower claim may survive; a chain may require Stop; or the record may reveal Non-Capture. Those effects require the later dimension and integrated audits.

#### C47-WP1-HANDOFF-01 — WP1 hands WP2 a dimension-specific reference architecture without adjudication

WP1 now establishes:

- three distinct continuity dimensions: Reference, Functional, and Temporal where applicable;
- `type continuity` only as a continuity view under canonical `TypeIntegrity`;
- dimension-specific declarations rather than one binary continuity result;
- Reference Continuity as continued identifiability of the same historical or structural referent through preserved constitutive relations;
- the same-referent burden through explicit object, process, relation-bundle, preservation-criterion, alternative, and Loss declarations;
- nominal sameness and label persistence as insufficient evidence;
- Reference Discontinuity where the object, process, or identity-bearing relation bundle is replaced or severed;
- mixed local findings without premature chain or Output mapping;
- occurrence-level PMS reference control without operator-type modification.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
N0:
  status: nominal_sameness_assertion_not_accepted
CR:
  result: unadjudicated
CT:
  result: unadjudicated
CF:
  result: unadjudicated
CTM:
  result: unadjudicated
CC:
  result: unadjudicated
continuity_audit:
  route_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
```

WP2 receives the dimension architecture and the reference findings. It must operationalize origin-type preservation under `TypeIntegrity`, type-discontinuity forms, Functional Continuity, Functional Discontinuity, source-function dependence, and the analogy boundary. It must not adjudicate `N0`, `CR`, `CT`, `CF`, `CTM`, or `CC`; select a chain result; pre-empt WP3 Temporal Continuity and continuity-matrix procedures; or map the pressure packet to a canonical Output Class.
<a id="47-4-type-continuity-as-a-typeintegrity-view"></a>

### 47.4 Type Continuity as a TypeIntegrity View

Type continuity names a practical continuity question under the already canonical `TypeIntegrity` Rule: does the transformation preserve the declared typing of its source objects, occurrences, components, composites, and derived functions, or does it warrantably revise a prior typing without silently replacing one object kind with another? The phrase is useful because type preservation must often be inspected across several operation steps. It does not create a new Rule, a second type system, or an independent continuity dimension alongside Reference, Functional, and Temporal Continuity.

```text
type continuity
=
continuity view under TypeIntegrity

not

a seventeenth Rule
an automatic sameness claim
a license to retype through function
```

A type-continuity view begins from explicit source typing. The record identifies whether the source is an operator occurrence, configuration, event, Non-Event, transition, sequence, path, trajectory, composite, or another already defined STRATA object. It then states what the operation may produce and which type distinctions must remain visible after the transformation.

#### C47-TYPECONTINUITY-01 — Origin typing is preserved or warrantably revised only under canonical TypeIntegrity

A minimally inspectable type-continuity view declares:

```yaml
type_integrity_continuity_view:
  source_object:
  source_origin_type:
  source_operator_occurrence_typing:
  operation:
  component_types_where_applicable:
  composite_type_where_applicable:
  target_object:
  bounded_target_function_where_applicable:
  asserted_type_preservation_or_revision:
  revision_basis_where_applicable:
  prohibited_identity_collapse_checked:
  limits:
```

A **preserved** type finding means that the target representation continues to identify the origin type and any relevant component or occurrence typing. A **warranted revision** is possible only where the prior typing itself is corrected or narrowed on the basis of the source and the canonical object model. Revision does not mean that a later analytical convenience may overwrite the source type. It creates a new testable typing claim and must state what was wrong, incomplete, or overbroad in the earlier claim.

```text
warranted type revision
≠ target-function substitution
≠ composite inheritance
≠ label replacement
```

The three operations impose different preservation burdens.

For `COMPOSE`, source components retain their own types while the operation produces a new composite analytical object. The composite may have a declared composite type, but that type does not flow backward into every component. Nor does one component's type automatically become the type of the whole.

```text
component types retained
+ declared constitutive relations
→ new composite object

new composite type
≠ retroactive component retyping
```

For `DECOMPOSE`, the source must be a concrete occurrence or composite. The operation may reconstruct finer components and relations, but it does not decompose the abstract Δ–Ψ operator type. If a □-typed occurrence is opened under finer granularity, the output may reveal practices, relations, events, or other occurrences that constitute that occurrence. It does not reveal smaller pieces of the operator type □ itself.

```text
operator occurrence or composite
→ possible DECOMPOSE source

operator type
→ never DECOMPOSE source
```

For `PROJECT_AS`, the origin type remains visible while the source object performs a bounded function in a declared target context. A trajectory may function as a frame; a composite may function as an attractor; a documented relation bundle may function as a boundary condition. None of those contextual functions replaces the origin type.

```text
origin-typed source object
+ bounded target function
→ two retained declarations

origin type
≠ target function
```

#### C47-ORIGINTYPE-01 — Operation-specific type boundaries remain explicit

The operation-specific burden can be summarized as follows:

| Operation | Type declaration that must remain visible | Prohibited collapse |
|---|---|---|
| `COMPOSE` | component types and the newly declared composite type | component = composite automatically |
| `DECOMPOSE` | source occurrence/composite type and finer reconstructed object types | operator type = decomposable occurrence |
| `PROJECT_AS` | source origin type and bounded contextual target function | target function = origin type |

This table does not determine whether an operation is admissible. It controls the type claims that must be available for audit. Reference Continuity, Functional Continuity, Temporal Continuity, TraceableLoad, Loss, and the remaining LIMITS burdens still require separate findings.

PMS typing remains occurrence-based. A transformation may preserve or revise the typing of a concrete operator occurrence where the source warrants that result. It may not rename, reorder, decompose, extend, or replace the Δ–Ψ operator types or their dependencies. A derived profile, composite pattern, or contextual function remains derived unless PMS Base already defines it as a primitive.

```text
derived object or function
≠ new PMS primitive

recurrent occurrence profile
≠ operator type
```

<a id="47-5-type-discontinuity"></a>

### 47.5 Type Discontinuity

Type Discontinuity occurs where the transformation's target can no longer be read without replacing, suppressing, or confusing the source object's canonical typing. It is not established merely because a new target object is produced. `COMPOSE` necessarily produces a composite, `DECOMPOSE` produces finer reconstructed objects, and `PROJECT_AS` declares a contextual function. The discontinuity lies in an illicit identity claim between types, not in transformation as such.

#### C47-TYPEDISCONTINUITY-01 — Forbidden type replacement, inheritance, and promotion are explicit failure forms

Typical forms include:

1. **Origin-type overwrite** — a `PROJECT_AS` target function is stated as though it were the source object's origin type.
2. **Composite-to-component inheritance** — each component is assigned the type or property of the composite merely because it participates in the whole.
3. **Component-to-composite inheritance** — the whole is assigned one component's type without a constitutive composition warrant.
4. **Operator-type decomposition** — an abstract PMS operator type is treated as though it were a concrete occurrence or composite that can be opened into parts.
5. **Derived-profile promotion** — a recurrent pattern, trajectory profile, composite effect, or contextual function is promoted to a new PMS primitive.
6. **Label-preserving retyping** — the same name or operator label is retained while the underlying object kind has changed.
7. **Cross-context type drift** — a target function used in one context is carried into another as a stable object type without a new projection test.

The audit question is:

```text
Which canonical source type was declared,
which target type or function is now asserted,
and what warrant permits the relation without identity collapse?
```

A Type Discontinuity finding must name the exact collapse. General discomfort with a metaphor or a changed vocabulary is not enough. Conversely, a smooth or familiar label cannot repair the collapse.

```text
semantic fluency
≠ TypeIntegrity

repeated target label
≠ preserved origin type
```

Type Discontinuity may have different later claim effects. A broad claim may require reduction while a narrower bounded function survives. A useful resemblance may remain `analogy_only`. A chain may require Stop because a later operation depends on the overwritten type. In other circumstances, the transformation may fail. Chapter 47 does not map these possibilities mechanically and does not select one for the synthetic pressure packet.

A later corrected type claim does not erase the earlier discontinuity.

```text
new bounded typing claim
= new testable claim

later corrected typing
≠ earlier type replacement erased
```

<a id="47-6-functional-continuity"></a>

### 47.6 Functional Continuity

Functional Continuity asks whether the bounded function claimed at the target remains dependent on concrete features and relations of the source object within the declared context. The function need not reproduce the source object and need not be available in every context. It must, however, remain reconstructibly carried by the source.

```text
source object X
+ concrete load-bearing features F
+ constitutive relation R
+ target context C
→ bounded function Y within C
```

The question is not whether the target wording sounds appropriate. It is whether the source features identified in the record continue to make a difference to the function claimed.

#### C47-FUNCTIONALCONTINUITY-01 — A bounded target function remains source-dependent and context-bound

A Functional Continuity declaration includes:

```yaml
functional_continuity_view:
  source_reference_object:
  origin_type:
  target_function:
  target_context:
  validity_scope:
  constitutive_source_features:
  constitutive_source_relations:
  source_target_dependency:
  relevant_counterfactual_pressure:
  alternatives:
  loss_and_uncertainty:
  local_finding:
```

The target function may remain continuous through substantial compression or through changes in non-load-bearing details. It may also narrow where only part of the original functional claim remains traceable. What cannot remain invisible is the dependency relation by which the source carries the target function.

Functional Continuity is therefore compatible with several bounded forms:

- a stable function supported by a preserved invariant across source variation;
- a function preserved only for a narrower context or time range;
- a function whose strength is reduced because part of the Source Trace is uncertain;
- a function retained while Reference or Temporal Continuity remains separately limited;
- a function unavailable because the relevant source relation no longer exists.

These are local possibilities, not automatic Output mappings.

For `PROJECT_AS`, Functional Continuity is central because the operation explicitly assigns a bounded target function. For `COMPOSE`, it matters where the composite is claimed to perform a function not attributable to any component alone. For `DECOMPOSE`, it matters where finer reconstruction is expected to return to or explain a coarser function of the same reference object.

```text
functional continuity
≠ identical surface form
≠ universal availability
≠ causal proof
```

#### C47-FUNCTIONDEPENDENCE-01 — Functional Continuity uses Source Trace and Sensitivity without redefining them

Chapter 45 owns the detailed Constitutive Source Trace and Source-Target Dependency procedures. Chapter 46 owns the six-value Counterfactual Sensitivity procedure. Chapter 47 uses their warranted local findings to test continuity of function.

The dependence burden is:

```text
identified source feature or relation
+ mapped target-function element
+ source-target dependency warrant
+ bounded sensitivity pressure where testable
→ possible Functional Continuity
```

This does not require that every source detail be preserved. It requires that the function's load-bearing source path remain visible. Where Counterfactual Sensitivity is available, a relevant change in the declared source relation should place bounded pressure on the function. Where the test is `underdetermined` or `untestable`, Chapter 47 retains that limit rather than treating the function as either continuous or discontinuous by default.

```text
untestable dependence
≠ Functional Continuity presumed
≠ Functional Discontinuity proved
```

Likewise, an `insensitive` local finding is not self-interpreting. It may indicate a genuine invariant, source substitutability, an overbroad function label, or a badly specified test. Chapter 47 may use an already warranted interpretation; it does not repeat or override the Chapter-46 procedure.

<a id="47-7-functional-discontinuity"></a>

### 47.7 Functional Discontinuity

Functional Discontinuity occurs where the target function no longer depends on the source features asserted to carry it, where the target can be transferred across materially different contexts without a preserved invariant, or where the claim survives only as resemblance or rhetoric.

Typical forms include:

- source features are named but no target element depends on them;
- the constitutive relation is replaced by a generic thematic association;
- the same function label is applied to materially opposed sources without an invariant;
- the target context changes while the function is treated as context-independent;
- the origin object is no longer identifiable, yet its function label persists;
- a projected function is carried into a chain after its Source Trace has been severed;
- a decomposition produces details that cannot return to the claimed source function;
- a composite is assigned a macrofunction while the relations that could carry that function are excluded.

#### C47-FUNCTIONALDISCONTINUITY-01 — Source-independent, metaphor-only, or context-interchangeable function claims break continuity

A Functional Discontinuity finding must identify the failed dependency rather than merely state that the projection is weak. The record asks:

```text
Which concrete source feature or relation was required,
which target-function element depended on it,
and where has that dependence become absent,
interchangeable, metaphorical, or unreconstructible?
```

A target function that remains verbally stable across materially different sources may be robust, but only where an invariant or alternative load path is reconstructible. Without such a warrant, stability may indicate Macro-Label Elasticity or source-independent function assignment. The result remains local and must be integrated with Reference, TypeIntegrity-view, Temporal, Loss, calibration, Stop, and Non-Capture findings later.

Functional Discontinuity is also context-relative. A source object may warrant a function in context `C1` but not `C2`. Carrying the label from `C1` to `C2` is a new `PROJECT_AS` claim, not a continuation by default.

```text
function warranted in C1
≠ function warranted in C2 automatically

new context
= new testable projection claim
```

#### C47-ANALOGYBOUNDARY-01 — Bounded function remains distinct from semantic analogy

Analogy can be analytically useful without satisfying Functional Continuity. The key distinction is:

```text
shared resemblance or interpretive fit
→ possible analogy

constitutive source relation
+ bounded target context
+ source-dependent target function
→ possible valid projection
```

Where only resemblance remains, the method must not silently retain the stronger function claim. `analogy_only` remains a later possible canonical mapping after the complete audit. It is not selected automatically by one local Functional Discontinuity finding, because the record may instead support Claim Reduction, a narrower projection, Stop, failure, or Non-Capture.

```text
functional discontinuity
≠ analogy_only automatically

analogy retained
≠ origin type changed
```

This boundary protects both sides. It prevents a metaphor from being presented as a source-dependent function, and it prevents a useful analogy from being discarded merely because it is not a valid `PROJECT_AS` result.

#### C47-WP2-HANDOFF-01 — WP2 hands WP3 separate type-view and function findings without chain adjudication

WP2 now establishes:

- type continuity only as preservation or warranted revision under canonical `TypeIntegrity`;
- explicit operation-specific type burdens for `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS`;
- preservation of component types, composite typing, source occurrence boundaries, origin type, and bounded target function without identity collapse;
- Type Discontinuity through overwrite, inheritance, operator-type decomposition, derived-profile promotion, and cross-context drift;
- Functional Continuity as source-dependent, context-bound, and claim-scoped;
- use of Constitutive Source Trace, Source-Target Dependency, and Counterfactual Sensitivity without redefining Chapters 45–46;
- Functional Discontinuity where the function becomes source-independent, metaphor-only, or context-interchangeable;
- the distinction between bounded function and analogy, with `analogy_only` retained only as a later possible mapping;
- continued occurrence-level PMS discipline without alteration of Δ–Ψ types or dependencies.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
N0:
  status: nominal_sameness_assertion_not_accepted
CR:
  result: unadjudicated
CT:
  result: unadjudicated
CF:
  result: unadjudicated
CTM:
  result: unadjudicated
CC:
  result: unadjudicated
continuity_audit:
  continuity_result_selected: false
  chain_result_selected: false
  route_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
```

WP3 receives separate Reference, TypeIntegrity-view, and Functional findings. It must operationalize Temporal Continuity, reject exhaustive chronology, construct the dimension-specific continuity matrix, preserve partial and mixed findings, specify bounded claim effects, map the view to existing Shared-Record fields, and hand Chapters 48–53 their independent burdens. It must not adjudicate `N0`, `CR`, `CT`, `CF`, `CTM`, or `CC`; select a chain result, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, or canonical Output Class; or allow a local type or function finding to stand for the complete continuity audit.

<a id="47-8-temporal-continuity"></a>

### 47.8 Temporal Continuity

Temporal Continuity asks whether the order, duration, recurrence, sedimentation, turning points, Non-Events, and irreversible load required by the declared claim remain reconstructible through the transformation. It is required wherever the source object is PATH-derived, historically formed, sequence-sensitive, or otherwise dependent on temporal relations that cannot be exchanged without changing the claim.

The relevant burden is not that every dated occurrence be retained. It is that the transformation preserve enough of the claim-bearing temporal structure to sustain the same reference, type-view, or function claim.

```text
same elements
+ altered order, duration, recurrence, or historical load
→ possibly different object, function, or claim
```

Temporal Continuity is therefore distinct from chronological similarity. Two records may contain the same listed events while differing in the temporal relations that make those events constitutive. Conversely, a compressed reconstruction may omit many dates and still preserve the order, delay, recurrence, and turning points on which the claim depends.

#### C47-TEMPORALCONTINUITY-01 — Temporal Continuity preserves claim-bearing order, duration, sedimentation, turning points, Non-Events, irreversibility, and historical load

A Temporal Continuity declaration must identify the temporal structures that carry the claim rather than merely state that the source is historical. Depending on the object and Claim Scope, these may include:

- order or sequence among constitutive occurrences;
- duration, persistence, or latency;
- recurrence and interval structure;
- accumulation or sedimentation;
- branching, threshold, or turning-point relations;
- expected but absent events whose absence is structurally load-bearing;
- irreversible commitments, exclusions, or path formation;
- historically inherited costs, capacities, roles, or asymmetries;
- temporal relations connecting local occurrences to a wider function.

The minimal view is:

```yaml
temporal_continuity_view:
  reference_object:
  temporal_scope:
  claim_bearing_temporal_structures:
    order:
    duration:
    recurrence:
    sedimentation:
    turning_points:
    non_events:
    irreversibility:
    historical_load:
  preservation_criterion:
  preserved_temporal_trace:
  compressed_temporal_trace:
  excluded_temporal_trace:
  uncertain_temporal_trace:
  irrecoverable_temporal_loss:
  claim_effect:
  local_finding:
```

This conceptual view reuses existing temporal, continuity, source, Loss, and claim fields. It does not introduce new top-level fields or a second Loss vocabulary.

For a PATH object, sequence must remain distinct from path, trajectory, and path dependence. Temporal Continuity cannot be inferred merely because the target preserves an ordered list. A path claim requires the relations by which earlier occurrences constrain or condition later possibilities. A trajectory claim requires the declared pattern across the relevant series. Path dependence requires the stronger historical load specified elsewhere in the corpus. Chapter 47 preserves these distinctions; it does not redefine them.

Operation-specific pressure remains asymmetric:

```text
COMPOSE
→ preserve claim-bearing order, interval, branch, and historical load
  across the new composite

DECOMPOSE
→ preserve the source object's temporal identity while opening
  finer occurrences or relations

PROJECT_AS
→ preserve the temporal source relation that carries the bounded
  target function within the declared context
```

A `COMPOSE` result may fail Temporal Continuity where it rearranges occurrences for narrative convenience, erases a decisive delay, converts recurrence into a single event, or combines pre- and post-threshold structures as though they were simultaneous. A `DECOMPOSE` result may fail where finer slices are detached from the sequence or sedimentation that made them parts of the same occurrence. A `PROJECT_AS` result may fail where a function formed through duration, repetition, or irreversible commitment is treated as immediately available in another context.

The PMS relation remains occurrence-based. A temporal relation among concrete Δ–Ψ-typed occurrences may carry historical load. The abstract order of PMS operator types or `operator_layers` does not by itself establish Temporal Continuity for an empirical or analytical object.

```text
PMS dependency order
≠ historical sequence of the reference object

operator-type order
≠ occurrence-level temporal trace
```

No operator type, dependency, layer, or primitive is changed by a temporal-continuity test.

#### C47-NOEXHAUSTIVECHRONOLOGY-01 — Temporal Continuity does not require exhaustive or lossless chronology

Temporal Continuity is compatible with declared compression. The method does not require a transformation to reproduce every date, event, interval, document, or intermediate state. It requires the preservation of the temporal relations necessary for the declared Claim Scope.

```text
non-exhaustive chronology
+ preserved claim-bearing temporal relations
+ explicit Loss
→ possible Temporal Continuity
```

The inverse also holds:

```text
exhaustive chronology
+ missing constitutive order or historical load
→ no Temporal Continuity by volume alone
```

A timeline can therefore be detailed yet temporally inadequate, while a concise sequence can be temporally adequate for a bounded claim. Detail count, temporal precision, and chronology length do not compensate for erased turning points, Non-Events, delays, sedimentation, or irreversibility.

The required compression disclosure remains the canonical five-part Loss declaration:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

Temporal Continuity and Loss are related but not identical. Chapter 47 asks whether the claim-bearing temporal relation remains reconstructible. Chapter 48 owns the complete procedure for selection, compression, exclusion, uncertainty, irrecoverability, and compression debt. A transformation may preserve Temporal Continuity while carrying substantial declared Loss; it may also lose Temporal Continuity through one small but constitutive omission.

Where the sources do not permit reconstruction of the necessary temporal relation, the result remains source-limited, uncertain, or capture-limited as appropriate. Missing chronology does not prove absence of temporal load, and formal ordering cannot invent a historical relation that the sources do not support.

<a id="47-9-continuity-matrix-and-partial-continuity"></a>

### 47.9 Continuity Matrix and Partial Continuity

Chapter 47 requires a dimension-specific matrix because continuity findings need not rise or fall together. Reference Continuity, the TypeIntegrity view, Functional Continuity, and Temporal Continuity answer different questions and can therefore produce mixed local results.

#### C47-CONTINUITYMATRIX-01 — The continuity matrix records separate dimension findings and retains type only as a TypeIntegrity view

The canonical conceptual view is:

```yaml
continuity:
  reference:
    finding:
    preserved:
    limits:
    claim_effect:
  type_integrity_view:
    finding:
    preserved:
    limits:
    claim_effect:
  function:
    finding:
    preserved:
    limits:
    claim_effect:
  temporal:
    finding:
    preserved:
    limits:
    claim_effect:
  chain_continuity:
    result: unselected
  route:
    selected: false
  output_class:
    selected: false
```

The `type_integrity_view` entry applies canonical `TypeIntegrity`; it is not a new Rule. The matrix does not average local findings, assign weights, or calculate a continuity score. It preserves each dimension's source basis, Preservation Criterion, limits, Loss, and Claim Effect until the integrated audit determines what follows.

A local finding may be stated in bounded terms such as:

```text
preserved
preserved_with_limit
narrowed
partial
uncertain
source_limited
discontinuous
untestable where an inherited test is unavailable
```

These are descriptive local findings, not additions to the ten canonical Output Classes. Every final local or chain result must still map to the existing canonical inventory after all applicable gates have been applied.

#### C47-PARTIALCONTINUITY-01 — Continuity dimensions may pass, fail, narrow, or remain uncertain independently

Mixed continuity is not an exception. It is a normal possibility of transformed analytical objects.

Examples include:

```text
Reference Continuity preserved
+ TypeIntegrity preserved
+ Functional Continuity failed
+ Temporal Continuity partial
```

```text
Reference Continuity narrowed
+ TypeIntegrity preserved
+ Functional Continuity preserved within C1
+ Temporal Continuity source-limited
```

```text
Reference Continuity failed
+ target function retained only as analogy
+ origin type preserved
+ temporal finding not applicable to the reduced claim
```

No single local pass immunizes the other dimensions. No single local failure automatically determines the complete chain result. The effect depends on what the claim required.

For example, a bounded comparison may survive uncertain Temporal Continuity if it does not assert historical formation. A path-dependence claim cannot. A function claim may survive a narrowed Reference Scope where the relevant source relation is still traceable. A claim of identity across institutional replacement cannot.

```text
partial continuity
≠ total validity
≠ total failure automatically
```

The matrix also preserves operation-chain locality. In a `COMPOSE → PROJECT_AS` chain, the composite may preserve its component and reference relations while the projection severs Functional Continuity. The first operation's local result remains visible. Conversely, a projection may preserve a bounded function while the preceding composition has already erased a necessary temporal relation. Later passage does not repair the earlier failure.

```text
local operation continuity
+ handoff continuity
+ later operation continuity
→ possible chain continuity
```

All three burdens must remain separately inspectable. Chain continuity is not obtained by majority vote among dimensions or operations.

#### C47-CLAIMEFFECTS-01 — Partial continuity produces bounded claim effects without mechanical Output mapping

Dimension findings constrain what may still be claimed. They do not mechanically select one canonical result. Possible bounded effects include:

- retention of the original claim where all required dimensions remain sufficiently continuous;
- narrower Reference Scope;
- narrower target context or validity period;
- separation of one composite claim into multiple claims;
- removal of an identity, necessity, universality, or persistence assertion;
- provisional status where a required dimension remains source-limited or uncertain;
- retention as `analogy_only` where resemblance remains but Functional Continuity does not;
- Claim Reduction where a supported residual claim remains after an overclaim is removed;
- Mandatory Stop where the current route cannot preserve a dimension required by the claim;
- `failed_transformation` where the transformation no longer reconstructs the declared object or function;
- Non-Capture where the available grammar or sources cannot adequately carry the relevant continuity structure.

These remain possible later mappings, not deterministic correspondences. In particular:

```text
one discontinuity
≠ failed_transformation automatically

partial temporal evidence
≠ admissible_but_provisional automatically

functional discontinuity
≠ analogy_only automatically

TypeIntegrity failure
≠ claim_reduction_required automatically
```

The complete result depends on Claim Scope, Source and Claim Ceilings, PraxisPurchase, TraceableLoad, Counterfactual Sensitivity, Loss, Anti-Immunization, Stop, Non-Capture, and the integrated audit.

Claim effects remain disposition-continuous:

```text
new narrowed continuity claim
= new testable claim

later bounded passage
≠ earlier discontinuity erased
```

A change of Frame, granularity, relative level, target context, composite, or function does not repair a prior continuity failure. It creates a new claim with its own Reference, TypeIntegrity-view, Functional, and Temporal burdens.

<a id="47-10-shared-record-view-claim-effects-and-handoff"></a>

### 47.10 Shared-Record View, Claim Effects, and Handoff

The continuity matrix is an integrated view of the existing Shared Transformation Record. It does not create a second record grammar, a new continuity object, or a separate chain schema.

#### C47-RECORDVIEW-01 — The continuity matrix maps to existing Shared Transformation Record fields

The matrix draws on existing declarations for:

- source and target reference objects;
- operation occurrence and operation chain;
- origin type, component types, composite type, and bounded target function;
- Frame, context, granularity, relative level, and temporal scope;
- Preservation Criterion and constitutive relation bundle;
- Constitutive Source Trace and Source-Target Dependency;
- Counterfactual Sensitivity where available;
- alternatives and counterpressure;
- the five-part Loss declaration;
- Source Ceiling, Claim Ceiling, and Claim Scope;
- local findings, Stop, Claim Reduction, Non-Capture, and final routing.

```text
continuity matrix view
≠ new Transformation Record
≠ new schema field
≠ new operation
≠ new Rule
≠ new audit stage
≠ new Output Class
```

The Formal Model may require separate dimension declarations, validate controlled vocabularies, check references, detect forbidden origin-type overwrite, and confirm that a matrix points to existing record paths. It may not determine historical identity from labels, decide that a temporal relation is constitutive, infer Functional Continuity from semantic fit, or choose the substantive chain result.

```text
schema-valid continuity view
≠ continuity proved
≠ chain admissibility proved
```

The matrix is also non-compensatory. Formal completeness, a strong local reference finding, or a fully populated source trace cannot compensate for a failed required continuity dimension.

#### C47-HANDOFF48-53-01 — Chapter 47 preserves the independent burdens of Chapters 48–53

Chapter 47 hands forward dimension-specific findings and bounded Claim Effects while preserving later ownership:

```text
Chapter 48
→ complete selection and Loss procedure

Chapter 49
→ Source Ceiling, Claim Ceiling, and calibration boundary

Chapter 50
→ Anti-Immunization across successor claims and transformed coordinates

Chapter 51
→ integrated Stop, Claim Reduction, and re-entry

Chapter 52
→ Non-Capture

Chapter 53
→ integrated twelve-stage audit and final routing
```

Chapter 47 does not convert a continuity matrix into a complete admissibility decision. It does not hide Loss in a `preserved` entry, treat source limitation as continuity, protect a successor claim from prior failure, select Stop or re-entry, or use Non-Capture to shield a weak claim.

The artifact-complete RETYPE lock remains `mandatory_stop`; the missing lock-critical packages remain `0/3`; and Part IV final lock remains unavailable. Continuity prose cannot repair absent artifacts or inherit authority from a chapter-level method result.

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

#### C47-HANDOFF-01 — WP3 hands WP4 a complete continuity method without adjudicating the pressure packet

Chapter 47 now establishes:

- separate Reference, Functional, and Temporal Continuity dimensions;
- `type continuity` solely as a view under canonical `TypeIntegrity`;
- same-referent and nominal-sameness boundaries;
- operation-specific origin-type, component/composite, and operator-occurrence protection;
- source-dependent and context-bounded Functional Continuity;
- the analogy boundary;
- claim-bearing Temporal Continuity without exhaustive chronology;
- a dimension-specific continuity matrix;
- independent partial and mixed findings;
- bounded Claim Effects without mechanical Output mapping;
- Shared-Record mapping and formal-model limits;
- preserved ownership for Chapters 48–53;
- continued PMS, inventory, model, person, and authority boundaries.

The synthetic pressure packet remains unchanged:

```yaml
N0:
  status: nominal_sameness_assertion_not_accepted
CR:
  result: unadjudicated
CT:
  result: unadjudicated
CF:
  result: unadjudicated
CTM:
  result: unadjudicated
CC:
  result: unadjudicated
continuity_audit:
  continuity_result_selected: false
  chain_result_selected: false
  route_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
```

WP4 receives a substantively complete Chapter 47. It must run the integrated Contract, redundancy, ownership, pressure-packet, record-view, inventory, PMS, model, and authority audit; verify all twenty-four duties exactly once; determine the bounded Chapter-level result without allowing it to flow to `N0`, `CR`, `CT`, `CF`, `CTM`, or `CC`; and prepare Chapter 48 without creating or adjudicating continuity, Loss, Case, Test, or Smoke artifacts.

<a id="chapter-48-compression-loss-and-reconstruction-selection"></a>

## Chapter 48 — Compression Loss and Reconstruction Selection

Chapter 48 makes explicit a burden already present in every STRATA operation: a transformation cannot carry everything that could be said about its source object, relations, coordinates, alternatives, and history into one result. It therefore selects. What it retains, condenses, omits, leaves unresolved, or can no longer reconstruct must remain visible as part of the claim rather than being treated as an accidental afterthought.

The chapter systematizes the existing five-part Loss declaration:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

These fields do not form an information ontology, a completeness score, or a second Transformation Record. They are common record paths through which operation-specific selection and Loss become inspectable. Their presence does not prove that Loss is admissible. Their absence cannot be excused by a clear target, a compact result, or a formally complete model.

The chapter uses one synthetic pressure packet introduced in the Preparation Record. It is method pressure only. `S0` remains an unaccepted losslessness assertion, while `LP`, `LC`, `LE`, `LU`, `LI`, `CD`, and `HL` remain unadjudicated. No Case, Test, Smoke, route, Stop, Claim Reduction, or Output Class is selected here.

<a id="48-1-no-transformation-without-selection"></a>

### 48.1 No Transformation without Selection

Every STRATA transformation is formed through a declared analytical cut. The source object may be richly documented, internally heterogeneous, temporally extended, multiply related, and available under more than one Frame or granularity. The transformation result nevertheless has a bounded object, operation, question, context, and Claim Scope. Selection is therefore unavoidable.

```text
bounded transformation result
→ some structures foregrounded
→ some structures retained differently
→ some structures not carried forward
```

This is not a defect added by poor execution. It follows from the fact that `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS` each produce a specific analytical result rather than a duplicate of the entire source basis.

#### C48-NOSELECTIONFREE-01 — No STRATA transformation is selection-free or lossless

A transformation may preserve every structure required by its bounded claim. It may even produce no known claim-defeating Loss. It is still not selection-free. The operation identifies which source objects, relations, periods, alternatives, and target coordinates enter the result and in what form.

```text
all claim-bearing structure preserved
≠ all available structure reproduced
≠ lossless transformation
```

The losslessness assertion `S0` is therefore not accepted as a general property of valid transformation. This does not adjudicate any concrete Loss candidate in the synthetic pressure packet. It establishes only the method-level boundary:

```text
valid transformation
≠ selection-free transformation
≠ lossless transformation
```

Selection and Loss also remain distinct. Selection describes how the analytical result is formed. Loss records what happens to structures relative to that formation. A selected source feature may be preserved, compressed, or later shown uncertain. An unselected alternative may be excluded, or its status may be unknown because the sources cannot establish whether it existed. The categories cannot be inferred from selection alone.

No claim of formal completeness changes this boundary. A record may populate every required field and still fail to identify a load-bearing omitted relation. Conversely, a concise record may adequately disclose the structures relevant to its bounded claim.

```text
formal field completion
≠ substantive Loss capture
```

#### C48-SELECTIONDISCOVERY-01 — Reconstruction selection is neither discovery nor arbitrariness

Selection is not discovery because the source does not arrive already partitioned into the one correct STRATA result. A source basis may support several bounded reconstructions at different granularities, temporal scopes, or target contexts. The operation must state which reconstruction is being formed and why.

Selection is also not arbitrariness. It remains constrained by:

- the declared reference object;
- the source basis and Source Ceiling;
- Frame, granularity, relative level, and temporal scope;
- the operation signature;
- PraxisPurchase and TraceableLoad;
- TypeIntegrity and continuity;
- alternatives and counterpressure;
- Claim Scope and Claim Ceiling;
- the five-part Loss declaration.

```text
selection ≠ discovery
selection ≠ unrestricted choice
selection = bounded reconstructive commitment
```

A different selection can be legitimate without proving the first selection wrong. It creates a different testable claim where the coordinates, source subset, relation bundle, periodization, or target function materially differ. Later reconstruction does not erase the earlier selection, its Loss, or its disposition.

```text
new reconstruction selection
= new testable claim

later bounded reconstruction
≠ earlier failure, Stop, uncertainty, or Loss erased
```

#### C48-OPERATIONSELECTION-01 — Each operation carries a distinct selection burden

The shared selection principle does not collapse the three operation signatures.

`COMPOSE` selects:

- which source structures enter the composite;
- their order or simultaneity;
- the constitutive relations among them;
- temporal periodization and relevant intervals;
- which alternatives or counterpaths remain visible;
- the analytical identity of the new composite object.

```text
many source structures
+ declared selection and constitutive relations
→ possible composite analytical object
```

A mere inventory is insufficient. Elements selected without their load-bearing relations may create a broad object that has more contents but less traceability.

`DECOMPOSE` selects:

- which internal differences are opened;
- which components or subrelations count as reconstructively relevant;
- the target granularity;
- internal thresholds or temporal divisions;
- which finer structures preserve the same reference object;
- the Source-Function Return by which the finer reconstruction remains answerable to the coarser occurrence or composite.

```text
compressed occurrence or composite
+ declared finer distinctions
+ same-reference burden
→ possible finer reconstruction
```

`DECOMPOSE` never selects components of an abstract PMS operator type. It opens concrete operator occurrences or composites where the source warrants such reconstruction.

`PROJECT_AS` selects:

- which source features are foregrounded;
- which source relations are claimed to carry the function;
- the target context;
- the bounded target function;
- the validity scope;
- which source features remain background conditions rather than function-bearing features.

```text
origin-typed source object
+ selected constitutive source features
+ bounded target context
→ possible target function
```

The origin type remains unchanged. Selection of a target-relevant source feature does not retype the object, create a PMS primitive, or establish the function through resemblance alone.

For PMS-typed occurrences, the selection burden remains occurrence- and relation-level:

```text
selected concrete Δ–Ψ occurrence or relation
→ possible claim-bearing source structure

operator type name, layer, dependency position, or label count
≠ substantive selection warrant
```

No Δ–Ψ type, dependency, order, layer, or primitive is modified by Chapter 48.

<a id="48-2-preservation"></a>

### 48.2 Preservation

`preserved` records the claim-bearing structures that remain available in the transformation result either explicitly or through a reconstructible functional, relational, reference, or temporal trace.

Preservation is not duplication. A source relation can be preserved without every descriptive detail being reproduced. A temporal formation can be preserved through its claim-bearing order and irreversibility without a complete chronology. A component can remain preserved in a composite where its identity and constitutive contribution are still inspectable.

```text
preserved
= retained in a form sufficient for the declared claim and return path

preserved
≠ reproduced exhaustively
≠ unchanged wording
≠ label persistence
```

#### C48-PRESERVATION-01 — Preservation means explicit or functional retention of claim-bearing structure

A `preserved` entry must identify what survives and why it remains sufficient for the declared claim. Depending on the operation, preservation may concern:

- a source object or component identity;
- a constitutive relation;
- order, duration, recurrence, or Non-Event structure;
- a cost, exposure, role, or asymmetry distribution;
- an alternative or counterpath;
- an origin type;
- a bounded source-function dependency;
- a validity or Claim-Scope limit.

A record should not merely state `structure preserved`. It should specify the retained structure and the target element it continues to support.

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
    - source_structure: relation_R
      retained_as: constitutive_relation_in_target_Q
      support: source_trace_ST
      scope: bounded_context_C
```

This is an illustrative view of existing record paths, not a schema addition.

Preservation may be partial and dimension-specific. A composite may preserve component identities but compress temporal intervals. A decomposition may preserve the reference object while leaving one internal relation uncertain. A projection may preserve origin type but fail to preserve the source-function relation. The five fields and the continuity matrix must therefore remain separately inspectable.

```text
preserved in one dimension
≠ no Loss in other dimensions
```

#### C48-PRESERVATIONTRACE-01 — Preserved structure must remain identifiable through a substantive trace

A preserved structure must be returnable to its source basis. The return path may use:

- Reference Continuity;
- a constitutive relation trace;
- Functional Continuity;
- Temporal Continuity;
- an explicit component-to-composite mapping;
- a declared source-to-target dependency.

Nominal persistence is insufficient:

```text
same label
≠ structure preserved

same operator type
≠ same occurrence preserved

same target function wording
≠ source-function relation preserved
```

For `COMPOSE`, preservation requires that load-bearing components and relations remain identifiable within the composite rather than disappearing into one undifferentiated macro-label.

For `DECOMPOSE`, preservation requires that the finer reconstruction can still be related to the same source object and that the decomposition has not merely produced supported fragments without the relation that made them parts of that object.

For `PROJECT_AS`, preservation includes origin type and the concrete source relation that carries the bounded target function. A stable target label with no recoverable source relation is not preservation.

Preservation does not itself establish admissibility. The retained structure may be insufficient, the claim may remain above the Source or Claim Ceiling, or an excluded relation may defeat the target. The entry records what survives; later checks determine what may be claimed from it.

<a id="48-3-compression"></a>

### 48.3 Compression

`compressed` records source structure that is not represented exhaustively in the transformation result, while its existence, difference, or possible relevance remains acknowledged and recoverable enough for the current claim.

```text
compressed
= non-exhaustive representation with disclosed retained burden

compressed
≠ absent
≠ irrelevant
≠ silently erased
```

Compression is unavoidable in many valid transformations. A trajectory condenses repeated configurations into a bounded temporal object. A composite represents several structures without reproducing every local detail. A decomposition presents selected internal relations rather than every possible finer distinction. A functional projection foregrounds source features relevant to one target context while leaving other supported features in the background.

#### C48-COMPRESSION-01 — Compression is acknowledged non-exhaustive representation

A compression declaration must answer at least:

- What source structures are represented in reduced form?
- Which claim-bearing relation remains preserved?
- Which detail is not reproduced?
- Why is the reduced representation sufficient for the present Claim Scope?
- Which alternatives or later questions could require reopening?
- What uncertainty or irrecoverability remains?

A compression entry may therefore be compact while still preserving a reconstructible relation:

```yaml
loss:
  compressed:
    - source_structure: repeated_local_variations
      retained_invariant: recurrence_relation_R
      omitted_detail: occurrence_specific_descriptions
      current_claim_effect: none_detected_within_Q
      reopening_condition: claim_requires_local_distribution
```

Again, this is an illustrative view, not a new field grammar.

Compression remains distinct from exclusion. A compressed structure is carried in reduced form and remains acknowledged. An excluded structure is deliberately not included in the result. The same source item cannot be assigned casually to both fields; the record must explain whether and how it survives.

```text
compression
→ reduced representation remains

exclusion
→ structure deliberately not included
```

A large compression can remain compatible with a bounded claim where the load-bearing relation is preserved, the source trace remains available, alternatives are disclosed, and the claim does not pretend exhaustive capture. This possibility is retained as required counterpressure but not adjudicated for the synthetic packet in WP1.

Compression also creates no presumption of invertibility. A reduced representation may permit later reopening from retained sources without allowing the original target to be mechanically reversed into every source detail. Compression Debt and irrecoverability belong to WP2–WP3 and are not selected here.

```text
reopening possible
≠ transformation invertible
≠ all compressed detail recoverable
```

<a id="48-4-exclusion"></a>

### 48.4 Exclusion

`excluded` records source structure deliberately not included in the transformation result. Exclusion is not a synonym for irrelevance, error, or absence. It is a bounded analytical decision that must remain contestable.

```text
excluded
= known or supported structure deliberately not included

excluded
≠ nonexistent
≠ disproved
≠ irrelevant by definition
```

#### C48-EXCLUSION-01 — Exclusion requires reason, scope, and claim-effect pressure

Every exclusion declaration must state:

1. the excluded structure or alternative;
2. the reason for non-inclusion;
3. the operation and coordinates under which it is excluded;
4. the declared Claim Scope for which the exclusion is proposed;
5. the possible effect if the excluded structure were restored;
6. whether the exclusion affects Reference, TypeIntegrity-view, Functional, or Temporal Continuity;
7. whether it conceals a counterpath, Non-Event, cost, role, asymmetry, or rival target function;
8. the source basis and uncertainty surrounding the exclusion.

```yaml
loss:
  excluded:
    - source_structure: alternative_path_A
      reason: outside_declared_temporal_scope
      scope: claim_Q_within_tau
      counterpressure: restore_A_and_retest_Q
      possible_claim_effect: narrowing_or_defeat_unselected
```

This remains a controlled view of existing fields.

Exclusion can be justified where a structure is outside the declared object, Frame, period, question, or Claim Scope and where its restoration would not alter the bounded claim. That conclusion must be tested rather than presumed. A small excluded relation can be claim-defeating where it carries the only counterpath, the decisive asymmetry, the non-occurrence on which a sequence depends, or the source feature necessary for a target function.

```text
small excluded structure
+ load-bearing relation
→ possible major claim effect
```

Conversely, exclusion of a large amount of non-load-bearing detail does not automatically invalidate the transformation. Quantity is not the criterion. The issue is which relation, alternative, temporal structure, or distribution the omitted material carries for the declared claim.

Exclusion also remains distinct from source limitation. If the sources cannot establish whether a structure existed, the record must not present it as a deliberate exclusion. Its status may belong under `uncertain`, inherited source loss, or later Non-Capture pressure.

```text
known and deliberately not included
→ possible exclusion

not established by source
→ not exclusion by default
```

The same discipline applies to PMS occurrences. A transformation may exclude a documented operator occurrence from a bounded reconstruction only with an operation- and claim-specific reason. It may not treat the occurrence as irrelevant merely because another operator label seems to summarize the scene. Operator type, layer, or dependency position cannot substitute for the excluded occurrence-level relation.

#### C48-WP1-HANDOFF-01 — WP1 hands WP2 the selection and first three Loss-field architecture without adjudicating the pressure packet

WP1 establishes:

- no transformation is selection-free or lossless;
- selection is bounded reconstruction rather than discovery or arbitrariness;
- each of the three operations carries its own selection burden;
- `preserved` identifies explicit or functional retention through a substantive trace;
- `compressed` identifies acknowledged non-exhaustive representation;
- `excluded` identifies deliberate non-inclusion requiring reason, scope, and claim-effect pressure;
- label persistence, operator typing, source volume, or formal completeness cannot substitute for substantive Loss tracing.

WP1 does not determine the status of `LP`, `LC`, or `LE` in the synthetic pressure packet. It also does not define or select `uncertain`, `irrecoverable`, Compression Debt, hidden Loss, Stop, Claim Reduction, or an Output Class. Those burdens remain for WP2, WP3, and the later integrated audit.

```yaml
loss_pressure_packet_after_wp1:
  S0: lossless_transformation_assertion_not_accepted
  LP: unadjudicated
  LC: unadjudicated
  LE: unadjudicated
  LU: unadjudicated
  LI: unadjudicated
  CD: unadjudicated
  HL: unadjudicated
  route_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
```

The artifact-complete RETYPE lock remains `mandatory_stop`; the required lock packages remain `0/3`; Part IV remains open; and Part IV final lock remains unavailable.

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

<a id="48-5-uncertainty"></a>

### 48.5 Uncertainty

`uncertain` records a structure, relation, sequence, alternative, attribution, or Loss status whose warrant cannot presently be settled at the precision required by the declared claim. It is not a residual container for inconvenient material and not a license to replace unresolved structure with a precise analytical placeholder.

```text
uncertain
= unresolved under the current source and reconstruction conditions

uncertain
≠ absent
≠ irrelevant
≠ excluded by choice
≠ irrecoverable automatically
```

Uncertainty may arise because the source basis is incomplete, internally inconsistent, temporally discontinuous, differently granular, or unable to discriminate among several supported reconstructions. It may also arise inside the current transformation where a relation can be retained only provisionally, where the boundary between two periods remains unstable, or where a composite contains a documented but unresolved internal heterogeneity.

#### C48-UNCERTAINTY-01 — Unresolved status may not be translated into false precision

A valid `uncertain` entry must identify:

- the structure or relation whose status is unresolved;
- the coordinate at which the uncertainty matters;
- the source basis and its limitation;
- the competing possibilities still supported;
- the target-claim element affected;
- the precision that cannot be warranted;
- the condition under which the uncertainty could be reduced, retained, or reclassified;
- the current Claim Scope and Claim Ceiling.

```yaml
loss:
  uncertain:
    - source_structure: relation_R
      unresolved_between:
        - reconstruction_A
        - reconstruction_B
      source_limit: records_do_not_discriminate
      claim_effect: scope_or_direction_unselected
      resolution_condition: additional_temporal_or_relational_evidence
```

This is an illustrative view of existing record paths, not a schema addition.

False precision occurs when the transformation produces a single exact order, boundary, weight, attribution, or target function although the sources support only a range, several alternatives, or no discriminating result. Formal notation cannot repair this problem:

```text
precise field value
+ unresolved source structure
≠ precise warrant
```

The correct response may be a bounded range, several preserved alternatives, a provisional relation, a narrower claim, or an explicitly unresolved result. Which canonical Output Class follows remains a later audit question. WP2 does not map `LU` or any other pressure candidate to an Output Class.

Uncertainty must also remain distinguishable from ordinary variation. A source object can contain documented heterogeneity without the reconstruction being uncertain about that heterogeneity. Conversely, a stable target label can conceal uncertainty where the relation carrying it has not been established.

For PMS-typed material, uncertainty may concern which concrete operator occurrence or occurrence relation is supported, how occurrences are ordered, or whether an occurrence is load-bearing for the declared claim. It cannot be resolved by selecting an operator type merely because the label is available.

```text
uncertain occurrence typing or relation
≠ permission to modify Δ–Ψ
≠ permission to infer an occurrence from a type label
```

#### C48-UNCERTAINTYIRRECOVERABILITY-01 — Uncertainty, irrecoverability, and source limitation remain distinct

Uncertainty means that the current record cannot settle among still-live possibilities at the necessary precision. Irrecoverability means that a required structure can no longer be reliably reconstructed under the declared source and record conditions. Source limitation may prevent the method from establishing which of these descriptions is warranted.

```text
several live possibilities remain
→ possible uncertainty

required relation cannot be reliably reconstructed
→ possible irrecoverability

sources cannot establish whether the relation existed or was lost
→ source-limited status, not forced classification
```

The method must not call a structure irrecoverable merely because it is presently inconvenient to recover, nor call it uncertain when the record already establishes that the relevant source relation has been destroyed or never recorded. Equally, it must not manufacture a distinction between uncertainty and irrecoverability where the source basis cannot carry that distinction.

This separation matters for later reopening. Uncertainty may be reduced by new evidence or a better reconstruction while preserving the prior unresolved disposition. Irrecoverability may permit only a bounded successor claim, an explicit Non-Capture result, or a Stop. The complete consequences belong to WP3 and Chapters 49–53.

<a id="48-6-irrecoverability"></a>

### 48.6 Irrecoverability

`irrecoverable` records information, relation, ordering, distribution, or source-to-target trace that cannot be reliably reconstructed under the current source basis and record conditions. It is stronger than non-exhaustive representation and different from deliberate exclusion.

```text
irrecoverable
≠ compressed
≠ excluded
≠ uncertain
≠ current-operation failure automatically
```

Irrecoverability may concern a missing interval, a destroyed relation trace, merged heterogeneous records, lost component identities, unrecoverable ordering, or a source-function dependency that cannot be reconstructed after abstraction. The entry must state what cannot be recovered, why, and which claim burden is affected.

#### C48-IRRECOVERABILITY-01 — Irrecoverable Loss must be located and temporally attributed

Every `irrecoverable` entry must distinguish at least:

1. **inherited source loss** — the information or relation was already unavailable before the current STRATA operation;
2. **prior-transformation loss** — an earlier recorded transformation compressed, merged, or excluded the structure beyond reliable return;
3. **current-operation loss** — the present transformation itself makes the structure unrecoverable;
4. **attribution underdetermined** — the record establishes unrecoverability but cannot warrant where it arose.

```yaml
loss:
  irrecoverable:
    - source_structure: local_ordering_relation
      location: inherited_source_loss
      reason: source_records_begin_after_relevant_interval
      current_operation_contribution: none_established
      claim_effect: historical_sequence_claim_not_available
```

This remains an illustrative use of existing fields.

The distinction prevents a current operation from being blamed for source destruction that predates it. It also prevents inherited loss from being hidden behind the apparent formal clarity of a new reconstruction.

```text
source relation unavailable before T
≠ T caused the loss

T exposes inherited irrecoverability
≠ T repairs the missing relation
```

A current transformation may still be inadmissible if it claims more than the surviving source load supports. But that is a claim-calibration or ceiling problem, not proof that the operation caused the original loss.

Irrecoverability is claim-relative without being arbitrary. A lost local ordering may defeat a path-sequence claim while remaining irrelevant to a bounded inventory claim. A destroyed component identity may defeat a decomposition return path while a coarse aggregate count remains supportable. The record must name the affected claim rather than declare the entire source object analytically unusable.

For `COMPOSE`, irrecoverability can arise when component identities, relation directions, temporal order, or rival paths are merged without a retained return path. For `DECOMPOSE`, it can arise when the decomposition fragments the source object and no longer permits reconstruction of the relation that made the components parts of that object. For `PROJECT_AS`, it can arise when the source features carrying the target function are no longer distinguishable from background features or alternative functions.

No operation-specific pattern creates a new Loss field or automatic Output Class. The exact five-field inventory remains closed.

<a id="48-7-reconstruction-selection"></a>

### 48.7 Reconstruction Selection

A STRATA reconstruction is formed through explicit analytical coordinates. The source basis may support more than one bounded reconstruction, and no single result is selected merely by being more detailed, broader, formally elegant, or closer to familiar PMS vocabulary.

```text
source basis S
+ declared coordinates K
+ operation-specific selection A
+ Claim Scope Q
→ candidate reconstruction R
```

The selection must be reconstructible. A reader must be able to identify which Frame, granularity, periodization, sources, relations, alternatives, and target functions were chosen and which relevant alternatives were not chosen.

#### C48-RECONSTRUCTIONSELECTION-01 — Reconstruction Selection must be operationalized across the full coordinate set

At minimum, the record must disclose:

- **Frame selection** — which boundary organizes the reference object and which plausible Frames are not used;
- **granularity selection** — which distinctions are retained and which finer or coarser resolutions remain available;
- **source selection** — which source materials carry the reconstruction and which relevant source classes are absent, excluded, or uncertain;
- **relevance selection** — which distinctions are treated as claim-bearing under the Praxeological Relevance Floor;
- **periodization selection** — which temporal boundaries, intervals, sequences, recurrences, Non-Events, or turning points define the object;
- **relation selection** — which constitutive, modulating, rival, or background relations enter the reconstruction;
- **Claim-Scope selection** — what the result may and may not support;
- **target-function selection**, where `PROJECT_AS` occurs — which bounded function is tested and which alternatives remain live.

These selections apply differently under each operation but remain subject to the same disclosure burden.

```text
same source basis
+ different declared coordinates
→ possible distinct bounded reconstructions

possible distinct reconstructions
≠ forced merger
≠ automatic ranking
```

A different selection may be more suitable for a different question without proving a universal hierarchy of Frames or granularities. Chapters 43–45 continue to govern scale-relative performance, PraxisPurchase, and TraceableLoad. Chapter 48 records how the selection shapes Loss.

#### C48-SELECTIONCOORDINATES-01 — Selected and rejected alternatives must remain visible at the relevant coordinates

A record must not disclose only the chosen path. It must identify the materially available alternatives against which the chosen reconstruction is bounded. This does not require an exhaustive universe of imaginable possibilities. It requires the supported or method-relevant alternatives that could change the claim.

```yaml
reconstruction_selection_view:
  selected:
    frame: C1
    granularity: g1
    periodization: tau_1
    relation_bundle: R1
    claim_scope: Q1
  alternatives_retained:
    - frame: C2
      possible_claim_effect: wider_scope_with_higher_loss
    - granularity: g2
      possible_claim_effect: local_asymmetry_visible
  alternatives_unavailable:
    - status: source_limited
```

This is a conceptual view of existing Shared-Record paths, not a new record grammar.

The selected/rejected distinction must remain exact. A source-supported alternative deliberately not used may belong under `excluded`. A selected alternative represented in reduced form may belong under `compressed`. An alternative whose status cannot be settled belongs under `uncertain`. An unavailable relation may be `irrecoverable` only where that stronger claim is warranted.

The method must also preserve prior dispositions. Selecting a new Frame, granularity, periodization, or target function creates a new testable claim. It cannot silently overwrite a failed or stopped prior reconstruction.

<a id="48-8-selection-effects"></a>

### 48.8 Selection Effects

Selection changes what becomes analytically visible, central, peripheral, comparable, or absent. These effects are not necessarily errors. They become methodologically dangerous when they remain undisclosed, strengthen a preferred reconstruction without warrant, or suppress a supported counterpath.

```text
selection effect
≠ bias automatically

undisclosed claim-strengthening selection effect
→ hidden-bias pressure
```

#### C48-SELECTIONEFFECTS-01 — Selection Effects must be tested for systematic foregrounding and concealment

At minimum, the transformation must pressure the following effects:

- **transition foregrounding** — one transition is treated as decisive while gradual formation or competing transitions disappear;
- **Non-Event concealment** — expected but absent occurrences are lost because only recorded events are selected;
- **asymmetry homogenization** — role, cost, access, exposure, or dependency differences are averaged into one composite description;
- **path linearization** — branches, reversals, delays, recurrence, or path dependence are rewritten as a single progression;
- **target-function privileging** — source features are selected because they fit one projected function while rival functions and origin-type burdens recede;
- **relation substitution** — visible elements are retained while the relation that made them claim-bearing is replaced by proximity, sequence, or label similarity;
- **periodization bias** — boundaries are placed where they strengthen the preferred claim and hide contrary intervals;
- **operator-label foregrounding** — a familiar Δ–Ψ type is selected as explanatory shorthand while concrete occurrence relations and alternatives disappear.

These are pressure questions, not automatic verdicts. A transformation may legitimately foreground one transition, period, relation, or function when the source trace, Claim Scope, alternatives, and Loss declaration warrant it.

#### C48-HIDDENBIAS-01 — Selection must be pressured against covert claim strengthening and counterpath suppression

Hidden-bias pressure asks whether the result would weaken, narrow, split, or fail if a supported alternative selection were restored. The test should examine at least:

- an alternative Frame;
- a plausible finer or coarser granularity;
- a rival periodization;
- an excluded counterpath or Non-Event;
- a documented asymmetry or heterogeneous distribution;
- a rival source relation or target function;
- the source object before operator-label compression.

```text
preferred reconstruction remains stable
only while supported alternative is suppressed
→ possible selection-bias pressure
```

The finding does not yet establish hidden Loss in the Chapter-48-WP3 sense. It identifies where Loss disclosure and later audit must concentrate. Nor does it require forced integration of every alternative. The correct result may be separate bounded reconstructions, a narrower claim, explicit uncertainty, a later Claim Reduction, Stop, or Non-Capture. Those outcomes remain unselected here.

A useful counterpressure is symmetry of burden:

```text
selected path must state why it is load-bearing
retained alternative must state how it could change the claim
rejected alternative must state why it is outside scope or unsupported
```

The method must not treat the selected reconstruction as the default truth and alternatives as optional objections. Selection is part of the claim and therefore part of the audit burden.

#### C48-WP2-HANDOFF-01 — WP2 hands WP3 all five Loss fields and the complete Selection architecture without adjudicating debt or hidden Loss

WP2 establishes:

- `uncertain` as unresolved source or reconstruction status that cannot be translated into false precision;
- `irrecoverable` as loss that cannot be reliably reconstructed under current source and record conditions;
- the distinction among uncertainty, irrecoverability, inherited source loss, current-operation loss, and source limitation;
- Reconstruction Selection across Frame, granularity, source, relevance, periodization, relation, Claim Scope, and target function where applicable;
- explicit selected and rejected alternatives at the relevant analytical coordinates;
- Selection Effects covering foregrounding, Non-Event concealment, asymmetry homogenization, path linearization, target-function privileging, and other claim-shaping effects;
- hidden-bias pressure without a hidden-Loss verdict.

WP2 does not adjudicate `LU` or `LI`, choose among `LP/LC/LE`, select Compression Debt or hidden Loss, apply Stop or Claim Reduction, choose a route, or assign a canonical Output Class.

```yaml
loss_pressure_packet_after_wp2:
  S0: lossless_transformation_assertion_not_accepted
  LP: unadjudicated
  LC: unadjudicated
  LE: unadjudicated
  LU: unadjudicated
  LI: unadjudicated
  CD: unadjudicated
  HL: unadjudicated
  route_selected: false
  stop_selected: false
  claim_reduction_selected: false
  output_class_selected: false
```

The artifact-complete RETYPE lock remains `mandatory_stop`; the required lock packages remain `0/3`; Part IV remains open; and Part IV final lock remains unavailable.

```yaml
governance:
  authority_inheritance: prohibited
```

<a id="48-9-compression-debt"></a>

### 48.9 Compression Debt

Compression can be locally adequate and still create a later analytical burden. `Compression Debt` names that burden. It arises where a bounded transformation legitimately reduces source detail for one claim, but later work requires reopening, revising, or narrowing the compressed result because a previously reduced structure becomes load-bearing for a new claim, a new operation, a chain handoff, or a newly available source basis.

```text
Compression Debt
= later reopening or revision burden generated by prior compression

Compression Debt
≠ proof that the prior transformation was invalid
≠ hidden Loss automatically
≠ irrecoverability automatically
```

Debt is therefore relational and temporally indexed. A record must identify which earlier transformation compressed what structure, for which claim the compression was accepted, and which later analytical demand makes the reduced structure newly consequential. Without those coordinates, “debt” becomes a retrospective label for any inconvenient result.

#### C48-COMPRESSIONDEBT-01 — Compression Debt is a later burden, not an automatic retroactive failure verdict

A Compression-Debt finding must declare at least:

1. the prior transformation occurrence;
2. the structure previously classified as `compressed`;
3. the prior Claim Scope and the reason the compression was then treated as bounded;
4. the later question, operation, source, Frame, granularity, or target function that requires reopening;
5. the claim-bearing relation that is no longer adequately visible;
6. the available reopening route;
7. remaining uncertainty and irrecoverability;
8. whether the later burden requires SUB reopening, PATH revision, RETYPE Claim Reduction, Stop, or Non-Capture pressure.

```yaml
compression_debt_view:
  prior_transformation: T0
  prior_claim_scope: Q0
  compressed_structure: relation_bundle_Rc
  prior_bounded_reason: sufficient_for_Q0
  later_trigger: new_claim_Q1
  newly_load_bearing_relation: R1
  reopening_route: unselected
  inherited_disposition_preserved: true
```

This is a view of existing record paths, not a new field or record type.

Compression Debt can arise under all three operations:

- after `COMPOSE`, where a later claim requires component identities, temporal order, rival paths, or internal asymmetries that the earlier composite compressed;
- after `DECOMPOSE`, where a later claim requires restoration of the coarser functional relation or source-object unity that was represented only minimally in the finer reconstruction;
- after `PROJECT_AS`, where a later target context requires source features, origin-type limits, or alternative functions that the earlier bounded projection did not reproduce in detail.

The debt does not show that the earlier operation should have preserved everything. Such a requirement would collapse bounded transformation into exhaustive reproduction. The relevant question is whether the earlier record disclosed the compression, calibrated its claim accordingly, and retained a sufficient return path or source reference for the claim then made.
